Linear-time logics – a coalgebraic perspective Thanks: Part of this work was funded by the Leverhulme Trust Research Project Grant RPG-2020-232.
Corina Cîrstea ORCID:0000-0003-3165-5678

Abstract
. We describe a general approach to deriving linear-time logics for a wide variety of state-based, quantitative systems, by modelling the latter as coalgebras whose type incorporates both branching and linear behaviour. Concretely, we define logics whose syntax is determined by the type of linear behaviour, and whose domain of truth values is determined by the type of branching behaviour, and we provide two semantics for them: a step-wise semantics akin to that of standard coalgebraic logics, and a path-based semantics akin to that of standard linear-time logics. The former semantics is useful for model checking, whereas the latter is the more natural semantics, as it measures the extent with which qualitative properties hold along computation paths from a given state. Our main result is the equivalence of the two semantics. We also provide a semantic characterisation of a notion of logical distance induced by these logics. Instances of our logics support reasoning about the possibility, likelihood or minimal cost of exhibiting a given linear-time property.

\ACMCCS
[
Theory of computation
]: Logic and verification; Modal and temporal logics;

1.
Introduction

Linear-time temporal logics such as LTL (see e.g.
[
Var95
]
) or the linear-time
\mu
-calculus (see e.g.
[
Var88
,
Dam92
]
), originally interpreted over non-deterministic models, have been successfully adapted to quantitative transition system models
[
Var99
,
FLS08
,
DV12
]
. These logics specify qualitative properties of paths in a (quantitative) transition system, and depending on the type of branching present in the models, have either a qualitative semantics (in the case of non-deterministic branching) or a quantitative one (in the case of probabilistic or weighted branching). Despite commonalities, which extend to the associated automata-based verification techniques, a general and uniform account of linear-time logics and their use in formal verification is still missing. Such an account would ultimately support the development of verification tools that are applicable to a wider class of models, thereby extending the scope of automated verification to include complex systems with a variety of quantitative features and correctness/optimality concerns. Extensions include (i) resource-aware systems, which associate
costs
to the different actions a system can take, and where the goal is to achieve the desired behaviour with minimal cost, and (ii) systems whose executions are
tree-shaped
, that is, an action can result in several successor states; the latter is needed e.g. to model systems whose structure evolves dynamically (see (
4
) of Example
2.2
).

The present paper makes some steps towards filling this gap, by studying linear-time, quantitative logics for a variety of models within a unifying framework. Specifically, we model systems as
coalgebras
whose type incorporates both
branching
and
linear
behaviour. These models come with a natural notion of
path
, which formalises an individual system execution. We study linear-time, fixpoint logics for such models, that are naturally induced by the model type. This builds on a recent coalgebraic account of maximal traces in systems with branching
[
Cîr17
]
. The branching type of a model is determined by a choice of quantitative domain for the transition weights, in the form of a partial semiring; this provides the domain of truth values for the logics, and dictates how the semantics quantifies over the branching. The linear type of a model describes the structure of individual transitions, and induces a
qualitative
notion of observable behaviour (of an individual execution), together with associated (both qualitative and quantitative) linear-time modal operators. The quantitative logics thus obtained have a natural step-wise semantics, as is standard in coalgebraic logics. However, this semantics does not directly capture the intuition that the (quantitative) interpretation of a formula measures the set of computation paths from a given state which satisfy the formula in the qualitative sense – the semantics makes no reference to computation paths. Formalising this intuition requires an extension of standard measure-theoretic concepts and results to measures valued into partial semirings. Once this is done, a more natural, path-based semantics can be defined, and proved equivalent to the step-wise semantics.

For this equivalence result to hold, it is necessary to omit standard propositional operators (conjunctions and disjunctions) from the logics. To see why this is necessary, consider the LTL formula
p\wedge q
, where is the LTL
next
operator. Its interpretation in existential LTL is ”there exists a path along which
p
is true in the next step and
q
is true in the next step”. Under a step-wise semantics, the interpretation of this formula would be a function of the interpretations of
p
and
q
. However, such a function cannot be defined: depending on the transition system, a path satisfying both
p
and
q
in the next step may or may not exist, when (potentially different) paths satisfying
p
and respectively
q
in the next step exist. The argument for the absence of arbitrary disjunctions is similar: in probabilistic LTL, there is no choice of interpretation for disjunctions of formulas which can compute the likelihood of
p\vee q
holding along paths from a given state, from the likelihoods of
p
, respectively
q
holding on these paths. While the absence of conjunctions and disjunctions from the syntax of the logics appears rather restrictive at first, this has relatively limited impact on the expressiveness of the logics: when interpreted qualitatively, our logics have the expressive power of
deterministic
parity automata; these, in turn, are as expressive as non-deterministic parity automata in the case of
word-like
linear behaviour, but less expressive than non-deterministic parity automata in the case of
tree-like
linear behaviour
[
TW68
]
(see also Remark
12
). As a result, as far as existential LTL and probabilistic LTL are concerned, our logics are equally expressive; in fact they are slightly
more
expressive, since LTL does not have the full expressive power of deterministic parity automata over infinite words.

Apart from matching the expressive power of existing linear-time logics for non-deterministic and probabilistic systems (see Example
6
), the logics we consider support reasoning quantitatively about the linear behaviour of resource-aware systems (see Example
2.1
), and of branching systems whose executions are tree-shaped (see Example
2.2
).

A more detailed outline of our approach and results is given below:

•

We model systems as coalgebras of endofunctors obtained by composing a
branching monad

{\mathsf{T}}_{S}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
with a
polynomial endofunctor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
. The branching monad arises from a partial commutative semiring
S=(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
, whose carrier provides the domain of truth values for a quantitative logic for
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras. Specifically, we take
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X=\{f:X\to S\mid f\text{ has finite support}\}
. Our logics contain both a (hidden) branching modality, used to quantify over the branching structure, and linear-time modalities, used to express properties of the linear behaviour. The semantics of the logics therefore requires
quantitative predicate liftings
to interpret both the linear-time modalities and the hidden branching modality. The forgetful logics of
[
KR16
]
are similar in their use of a hidden branching modality, although they are much more permissive in the choice of such a modality – they also cover logics which are expressive for bisimulation. The propositional operators employed by our logics are more restrictive than those found in linear-time temporal logics such as LTL
[
Var95
]
or probabilistic LTL
[
Var99
]
; nonetheless, our logics match the expressiveness of LTL, when instantiated to non-deterministic and probabilistic transition systems (see Example
6
), with the only impact on expressiveness occurring when linear-time behaviours are tree-shaped (see Example
3
).

•

In spite of their linear-time flavour, a key feature of our logics is their step-wise semantics: the interpretation of a formula is defined by successively unfolding the coalgebra structure, as required by the structure of the formula. This is different from logics such as (probabilistic) LTL or the (probabilistic) linear-time
\mu
-calculus, where the interpretation of a formula on a state is defined in terms of its interpretation on the (infinite) computation paths from that state. Moreover, unlike other coalgebraic logics (e.g. the forgetful logics of
[
KR16
]
), the canonical choices made in the semantics of our logics also allow for a (measure-theoretic) path-based semantics to be defined, and proved equivalent to the step-wise semantics. The path-based semantics relies on a coalgebraic notion of
path
through a coalgebra with branching, and involves defining a
\sigma
-algebra structure on the set of paths. Key to the equivalence result between the two semantics is the close relationship between the semiring structure and the associated branching modality.

•

In order to define our path-based semantics, a generalisation of standard measure extension results to semiring-valued measures is required. Specifically, we use a generalisation of the notion of
outer measure
(see e.g.
[
ADD99
,
RF10
]
) to extend a semiring-valued measure on a field of sets to a similar measure on the induced
\sigma
-algebra. Our focus is on the
existence
of such extensions; uniqueness is not of interest to us, since the measure extensions obtained via the use of outer measures fit our goal of providing an equivalent path-based semantics for our logics.

•

Our quantitative logics naturally give rise to a logical distance between states of coalgebras with branching, and the existence of a path-based semantics helps to provide a semantic characterisation of this logical distance. Specifically, we introduce the semantic notion of
linear-time distance
between states of two coalgebras, and show that it coincides with the logical distance.

We now comment on the relationship between our logics and existing quantitative, linear-time logics. We begin with the logic LTL, interpreted over either non-deterministic
[
Var95
]
or probabilistic transition systems
[
Var99
]
. In the case of non-deterministic systems, we consider the existential variant of LTL, whose semantics requires the existence of infinite computation paths satisfying certain linear-time properties. On the other hand, the probabilistic semantics of LTL uses measure theory to assign, to each linear-time property, the likelihood of it being satisfied on the infinite computation paths from a given state. A suitable choice of endofunctor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
allows us to recover the expressiveness of both existential LTL and probabilistic LTL, albeit using a slightly different syntax (see Example
6
). Our logics can therefore encode existing linear-time logics used in verification.

Other existing quantitative, LTL-like logics are either
weighted ones
, with weights taken from a strong bimonoid and with propositional operators interpreted using the bimonoid sum, respectively multiplication (see e.g.
[
DV12
]
), or
lattice-based ones

[
KL07
]
, with truth values taken from a lattice and with propositional operators interpreted using the lattice operations. The former approach immediately rules out partial semirings and does not appear to admit a path-based semantics, whereas the latter is much more restrictive than a semiring-based approach, given that the lattice idempotence laws do not generally hold in a semiring.

The remainder of the paper is structured as follows. Section
2
introduces partial semiring monads and semiring-valued relations and predicates, summarises previous work on a coalgebraic account of finite/maximal traces in systems with branching
[
Cîr17
]
, and recalls the basics of (qualitative) coalgebraic logics. Section
3
describes linear-time fixpoint logics for coalgebras with branching, along with their step-wise semantics. Section
4
introduces
semiring-valued measures
on
\sigma
-algebras, and studies the existence of measure extensions (from fields of sets to
\sigma
-algebras) in this setting. Building on this, Section
5
provides an alternative, path-based semantics for the linear-time fixpoint logics of Section
3
, based on notions of
maximal path
and
path fragment
in a coalgebra with branching, whereas Section
6
proves the equivalence of the two semantics. Finally, Section
7
shows that the logical distance induced by our logics coincides with a natural semantic distance, which itself has a linear-time flavour, while Section
8
summarises the results presented and briefly outlines future work.

Related Work

The logics considered here were originally introduced in
[
Cîr14
]
and further studied in
[
Cîr15
]
, where a path-based semantics for their
fixpoint-free
fragment was also described; the latter, however, did not employ any measure-theoretic machinery. The connection between the logics in
[
Cîr14
]
and parity automata was studied in
[
CSH17
]
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Monads and Partial Semirings

In what follows, we use
monads
on the category
{\mathsf{Set}}
of sets and functions to capture branching in coalgebraic types.

{defi}

A
monad
on a category
{\mathcal{C}}
is a tuple
({\mathsf{T}},\eta,\mu)
where
{\mathsf{T}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is a functor and
\eta:{\mathsf{Id}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{T}}
and
\mu:{\mathsf{T}}\circ{\mathsf{T}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{T}}
are natural transformations, called the
unit
and
multiplication
of
{\mathsf{T}}
, subject to the following laws:

\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}\eta}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{id}}_{\mathsf{T}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathsf{T}}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{id}}_{\mathsf{T}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathsf{T}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}}

The specific nature of the monads we consider makes them
strong
and
commutative
. A
strong monad
comes equipped with a
strength map

{\mathsf{st}}_{X,Y}:X\times{\mathsf{T}}Y\to{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)
, natural in
X
and
Y
and subject to coherence conditions w.r.t.
\eta
and
\mu
(see e.g.
[
Jac16
, Chapter 5]
for details). For such a monad, one can also define a
swapped strength map

{\mathsf{st}}^{\prime}_{X,Y}:{\mathsf{T}}X\times Y\to{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)
by:

\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{tw}}_{{\mathsf{T}}X,Y}}
\textstyle{Y\times{\mathsf{T}}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{st}}_{Y,X}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}(Y\times X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{tw}}_{Y,X}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)}

where
{\mathsf{tw}}_{X,Y}:X\times Y\to Y\times X
is the
twist map
taking
(x,y)\in X\times Y
to
(y,x)
.
Commutative monads
are strong monads where the maps
\mu_{X,Y}\circ{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{st}}^{\prime}_{X,Y}\circ{\mathsf{st}}_{{\mathsf{T}}X,Y}:{\mathsf{T}}X\times{\mathsf{T}}Y\to{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)
and
\mu_{X,Y}\circ{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{st}}_{X,Y}\circ{\mathsf{st}}^{\prime}_{X,{\mathsf{T}}Y}:{\mathsf{T}}X\times{\mathsf{T}}Y\to{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)
coincide, yielding a
double strength map

{\mathsf{dst}}_{X,Y}:{\mathsf{T}}X\times{\mathsf{T}}Y\to{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)
for each choice of sets
X,Y
.

The monads considered in this paper arise from partial commutative semirings.

{defi}

A
partial commutative monoid

(S,+,0)
is given by a set
S
together with a partial operation
+:S\times S\to S
and an element
0\in S
, such that:

•

s+0
is defined for all
s\in S
and moreover,
s+0=s
,

•

(s+t)+u
is defined if and only if
s+(t+u)
is defined, and in that case
(s+t)+u=s+(t+u)
,

•

whenever
s+t
is defined, so is
t+s
and moreover,
s+t=t+s
.

A
partial commutative semiring
is a tuple
S:=(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
with
(S,+,0)
a partial commutative monoid and
(S,\bullet,1)
a commutative monoid, with
\bullet
distributing over existing sums; that is, for all
s,t,u\in S
,
s\bullet 0=0
, and whenever
t+u
is defined, then so is
s\bullet t+s\bullet u
and moreover,
s\bullet t+s\bullet u=s\bullet(t+u)
.

The addition operation of any partial commutative semiring induces a pre-order relation
\sqsubseteq\,\subseteq S\times S
, given by

x\sqsubseteq y~~~\text{if and only if}~~~\text{there exists }z\in S\text{ such that }x+z=y

(1)

for
x,y\in S
. It follows immediately from the axioms of a partial commutative semiring that
\sqsubseteq
has
0\in S
as
bottom element
, that is,
0\sqsubseteq s
for all
s\in S
, and that
\sqsubseteq
is preserved by
+
and
\bullet
in each argument (see
[
Cîr17
]
for details).

{asm}

We assume that
(S,\sqsubseteq)
is a complete lattice, with the unit
1
of
\bullet
as top element. Moreover, we assume that both
+
and
\bullet
preserve joins of increasing countable chains, respectively meets of decreasing countable chains, in each argument.

Remark 1
.

Below we list some basic properties of a partial commutative semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
satisfying Assumption
2.1
:

(1)

Each
s\in S
has a (not necessarily unique) ”1-complement” w.r.t.
+
(that is, an element
s^{\prime}\in S
such that
s+s^{\prime}=1
). This follows directly from the definition of
\sqsubseteq
, using the fact that
1
is top for
\sqsubseteq
.

(2)

Whenever
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}
is defined (with
I
a finite set) and
J\subseteq I
,
\sum\limits_{i\in J}a_{i}
is also defined. This follows from
(S,+,0)
being a partial commutative monoid.

(3)

Whenever
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}
is defined (with
I
a finite set), then so is
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}\bullet b_{i}
. To see this, let
c_{i}\in S
be such that
b_{i}+c_{i}=1
for
i\in I
(by using (
1
) above). Then
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}=\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}\bullet(b_{i}+c_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\bullet b_{i}+a_{i}\bullet c_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\bullet b_{i})+\sum\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\bullet c_{i})
and therefore by (
2
),
\sum\limits_{i\in I}a_{i}\bullet b_{i}
is defined.

(4)

Let
a_{0}\sqsubseteq a_{1}\sqsubseteq\ldots
and
b_{0}\sqsubseteq b_{1}\sqsubseteq\ldots
be increasing chains in
S
with joins
a
and
b
, respectively. Then,
a+b
is the join of
a_{0}+b_{0}\sqsubseteq a_{1}+b_{1}\sqsubseteq\ldots
. That
a+b
is an upper bound for this increasing chain is immediate from the preservation of
\sqsubseteq
by
+
in each argument. That it is the least upper bound also follows easily: if
c
is an upper bound for
a_{0}+b_{0}\sqsubseteq a_{1}+b_{1}\sqsubseteq\ldots
then, for
i\in\omega
,
a_{0}+b_{i}\sqsubseteq a_{1}+b_{i}\sqsubseteq\ldots\sqsubseteq c
together with the preservation of joins of increasing countable chains by
+
in the first argument gives
a+b_{i}=\sup_{j\in\omega}a_{j}+b_{i}=\sup_{j\in\omega}(a_{j}+b_{i})\sqsubseteq c
; we then obtain
a+b=a+\sup\limits_{i\in\omega}b_{i}=\sup\limits_{i\in\omega}(a+b_{i})\sqsubseteq c
.

(5)

Similarly, if
a_{0}\sqsupseteq a_{1}\sqsupseteq\ldots
and
b_{0}\sqsupseteq b_{1}\sqsupseteq\ldots
are decreasing chains in
S
with meets
a
and
b
, respectively, then,
a+b
is the meet of
a_{0}+b_{0}\sqsupseteq a_{1}+b_{1}\sqsupseteq\ldots
. This is proved similarly to (
4
) above.

{exa}

In what follows, we consider the
boolean semiring

(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the (partial)
probabilistic semiring

([0,1],+,0,*,1)
, the
tropical semiring

{\mathbb{N}}=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
(with
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
) and its bounded variants
S_{B}=([0,B]\cup\{\infty\},\min,\infty,+_{B},0)
with
B\in\mathbb{N}
, where for
m,n\in[0,B]\cup\{\infty\}
we have

\displaystyle m+_{B}n=\begin{cases}m+n,\text{ if }m+n\leq B\\
\infty,\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

The associated pre-orders are
\leq
on
\{0,1\}
and
[0,1]
, and
\geq
on
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
and
[0,B]\cup\{\infty\}
. All these pre-orders satisfy Assumption
2.1
.

A partial commutative semiring
S
satisfying Assumption
2.1
induces a
semiring monad

({\mathsf{T}}_{S},\eta,\mu)
with

\displaystyle{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X

\displaystyle=\textstyle\{\,\varphi:X\to S\mid{\mathsf{supp}}(\varphi)\text{ is finite}\,,\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\varphi)}\varphi(x)\text{ is defined}\,\}\,,

\displaystyle({\mathsf{T}}_{S}f)(\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}f(x_{i})\text{ for }f:X\to Y,

\displaystyle\eta_{X}(x)(y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }y=x\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

\displaystyle\mu_{X}(\Phi)(x)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum\limits_{\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)}\Phi(\varphi)\bullet\varphi(x)\text{ for }\Phi\in{\mathsf{T}}_{S}^{2}X,

Here,
{\mathsf{supp}}(\varphi)=\{x\in X\mid\varphi(x)\neq 0\}
is the
support
of
\varphi
, and we use the formal sum notation
\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i}
, with
I
finite, to refer to the element of
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X
mapping

x\in X
to
\sum_{j\in J_{x}}c_{j}
with
J_{x}=\{i\in I\mid x_{i}=x\}
. To see that the sum
\textstyle\sum\limits_{\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)}\Phi(\varphi)\bullet\varphi(x)
used in the definition of the monad multiplication is always defined, note that since both
\textstyle\sum\limits_{\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)}\Phi(\varphi)
and
\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\varphi)}\varphi(x)
with
\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)
are defined, then by (
3
) of Remark
1
, so is
\textstyle\sum\limits_{\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)}\Phi(\varphi)\bullet(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\varphi)}\varphi(x))=\textstyle\sum\limits_{\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)}\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\varphi)}\Phi(\varphi)\bullet\varphi(x)
. It then follows by (
2
) of Remark
1
that for
x\in X
,
\textstyle\sum\limits_{\varphi\in{\mathsf{supp}}(\Phi)}\Phi(\varphi)\bullet\varphi(x)
is itself defined.

The monad
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
above is
strong
(as in fact all monads on
{\mathsf{Set}}
have this property) and
commutative
(see e.g.
[
CJ13
]
), with
strength

{\mathsf{st}}_{X,Y}:X\times{\mathsf{T}}_{S}Y\to{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(X\times Y)
and
double strength

{\mathsf{dst}}_{X,Y}:{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X\times{\mathsf{T}}_{S}Y\to{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(X\times Y)
given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{st}}_{X,Y}(x,\psi)(z,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\psi(y)&\text{if }z=x\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\qquad{\mathsf{dst}}_{X,Y}(\varphi,\psi)(z,y)=\varphi(z)\bullet\psi(y)\,.

We write
1=\{*\}
for a final object in the category
{\mathsf{Set}}
; this should not be confused with the unit of the semiring multiplication. We immediately note that
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}1=S
. In what follows, we will use
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}1
and
S
interchangeably, in particular
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}1
will be used in contexts where its (free)
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
-algebra structure (given by
\mu_{1}:{\mathsf{T}}_{S}^{2}1\to{\mathsf{T}}_{S}1
) is relevant.

The relationship between monads and partial semirings was thoroughly studied in
[
CJ13
,
Cîr17
]
. We use semiring monads to model branching, with the semirings in Example
2.1
accounting for finite non-deterministic, probabilistic and weighted branching. In the latter case, we think of the weights as costs associated to single computation steps, with the bounded variants of the tropical semiring imposing an upper limit on the maximum allowable costs.

Remark 2
.

Our earlier work
[
Cîr14
,
Cîr15
]
was parameterised by a so-called
partially additive monad
. The connection with the partial semiring monads used here is as follows: any commutative, partially additive monad which is, in addition, finitary, is isomorphic to a partial semiring monad (see
[
Cîr15
, Remark 2.4]
). While our earlier work also covers the unbounded powerset monad (as an additive monad), we are not aware of any other examples of non-finitary monads to which the results in
[
Cîr14
,
Cîr15
]
apply.

2.2.
Algebras and Coalgebras

{defi}

Given an endofunctor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
, an
F
-algebra
is a pair
(A,\alpha)
with
A
a set and
\alpha:FA\to A
a function, while an
F
-algebra homomorphism
between
F
-algebras
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
is given by a function
f:A\to B
such that
f\circ\alpha=\beta\circ Ff
. Also, an
F
-coalgebra
is a pair
(C,\gamma)
with
C
a set (of states) and
\gamma:C\to FC
a function called
transition map
, while an
F
-coalgebra homomorphism
between
F
-coalgebras
(C,\gamma)
and
(D,\delta)
is given by a function
g:C\to D
such that
Fg\circ\gamma=\delta\circ g
.

{exa}

(1)

When
F=A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
,
F
-coalgebras are in one-to-one correspondence with deterministic, labelled transition systems. Each state in such a system has a unique, labelled transition to another state.

(2)

When
F=\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
,
F
-coalgebras are in one-to-one correspondence with deterministic, labelled transition systems with termination. Each state in such a system has either a terminating transition or a unique, labelled transition to another state.

(3)

When
F=A\times{\mathsf{Id}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
, each state in an
F
-coalgebra has a unique, labelled transition resulting in
two
successor states.

(4)

When
F=\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}+B\times{\mathsf{Id}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
,
F
-coalgebras model (deterministic) systems whose execution in a given state can result in either termination, or a
single
successor state, or
two
successor states. The latter can be used to model systems whose structure can evolve dynamically: one can think of the second successor state as modelling the creation of a new process.

The coalgebras of interest to us are
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, where
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is a semiring-valued monad (see Section
2.1
) and
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is a
polynomial
functor (that is,
F
is constructed from identity and constant functors using
finite
products and set-indexed coproducts). We assume, w.l.o.g., that
F=\coprod_{i\in I}{\mathsf{Id}}^{j_{i}}
, with
j_{i}\in\omega
for
i\in I
. We note that any polynomial endofunctor on
{\mathsf{Set}}
is naturally isomorphic to a coproduct of finite (including empty) products of identity functors.

We write
(Z,\zeta)
for the
final

F
-coalgebra (that is, a final object in the category of
F
-coalgebras and
F
-coalgebra homomorphisms). Its existence, under the assumption that
F
is a polynomial functor, follows from
[
Rut00
, Theorem 10.1]
. We also write
(I,\iota)
for the
initial

F
-algebra (that is, an initial object in the category of
F
-algebras and
F
-algebra homomorphisms); its existence, for polynomial functors
F
, was proved in
[
Lam68
]
. Both
\zeta:Z\to FZ
and
\iota:FI\to I
are isomorphisms. We will refer to the elements of
Z
(
I
) as
maximal
(resp.
finite
)
traces
. Both finite and maximal traces are completed traces, with maximal traces including, in addition to the finite traces, also any infinite ones; this is illustrated in the next example.

{exa}

When
F=A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
, there are
no
finite traces (the initial
F
-algebra has an empty carrier), whereas maximal traces are given by infinite sequences of elements of
A
. Changing
F
to
\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
results in finite traces being given by finite sequences of elements of
A
(these capture terminating behaviours ending in
\iota_{1}(*)
), whereas maximal traces are given by either finite or infinite sequences of elements of
A
(the latter capture non-terminating behaviours).

A notion of
(maximal) path
from a state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra (similar to that in
[
Cîr11
]
), as well as a notion of
path fragment
from a state, are defined below.

{defi}

[Path, path fragment]

Let
F=\coprod_{i\in I}{\mathsf{Id}}^{j_{i}}
with
j_{i}\in\omega
for
i\in I
, and let
C
be a set (of states). A
path
is an element of the final
C\times F
-coalgebra
(Z_{C},\zeta_{C})
, while a
path fragment
is an element of the initial
C\times(\{*\}+F)
-algebra
(I_{C},\iota_{C})
. The
depth
of a path fragment
q\in I_{C}
is defined inductively by:

•

if
\pi_{2}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q))=\iota_{1}(*)
, then
0pt(q)=0
;

•

if
\pi_{2}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q))=\iota_{2}(\iota_{i}(*))
with
j_{i}=0
, then
0pt(q)=1
;

•

if
\pi_{2}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q))=\iota_{2}(\iota_{i}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{j_{i}}))
for some
i\in I
with
j_{i}>0
, then
0pt(q)=1+\max(0pt(q_{1}),\ldots,0pt(q_{j_{i}}))
.

A path thus corresponds to a possibly infinite tree, with each node annotated by a pair
(c,i)
with
c\in C
and
i\in I
, and having
j_{i}
immediate sub-trees. The leaf nodes in such a tree are of the form
(c,i)
with
j_{i}=0
. As
\iota_{C}:C\times(\{*\}+FI_{C})\to I_{C}
is an isomorphism, a path fragment is given by a
finite
tree, with non-leaf nodes similar to those in a path but with an additional type of leaf node, namely one annotated by elements of
C
only.

{defi}

A path fragment
q
is said to be a
prefix
of a path
p
if

(1)

\pi_{1}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q))=\pi_{1}(\zeta_{C}(p))
,

(2)

either
\pi_{2}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q))=\iota_{1}(*)
, or
\pi_{2}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q))=\iota_{2}(\iota_{i}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{j_{i}}))
for some
i\in I
and moreover,
\pi_{2}(\zeta_{C}(p))=\iota_{i}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{j_{i}})
and
q_{k}
is a prefix of
p_{k}
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
.

We write
{\mathsf{pref}}(p)
for the set
\{q\mid q\text{ is a prefix of }p\}
.

Thus,
q
is a prefix of
p
if the finite tree associated to
q
is a subtree of the possibly infinite tree associated to
p
. One can define when a path fragment
q
is a prefix of another path
fragment

p
in a similar way, namely by replacing the path
p
with a path fragment, and the use of
\zeta_{C}
by
\iota_{C}^{-1}
in Definition
2.2
. Two path fragments are then said to be
compatible
if they are both prefixes of some other path fragment, and are said to be
incompatible
otherwise.

{defi}

[Path, path fragment in
(C,\gamma)
]

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra. A
path from
c\in C
in
(C,\gamma)
is a path
p\in Z_{C}
such that

•

\pi_{1}(\zeta_{C}(p))=c
,

•

if
\pi_{2}(\zeta_{C}(p))=\iota_{i}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{j_{i}})
and
\pi_{1}(\zeta_{C}(p_{k}))=c_{k}
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
, then
\gamma(c)(\iota_{i}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{i}}))\neq 0
and moreover,
p_{k}
is a path from
c_{k}
in
(C,\gamma)
, for
k\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
.

A
path fragment from
c\in C
in
(C,\gamma)
is an element
q
of the initial
C\times(\{*\}+F)
-algebra
(I_{C},\iota_{C})
such that

•

\pi_{1}(\iota_{C}^{-1}(q))=c
,

•

either
\pi_{2}(\iota_{C}^{-1}(q))\!=\iota_{1}(*)
, or
\pi_{2}(\iota_{C}^{-1}(q))\!=\iota_{2}(\iota_{i}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{j_{i}}))
for some
i\in I
with
\pi_{1}(\iota_{C}^{-1}(q_{k}))\!=c_{k}
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
and
\gamma(c)(\iota_{i}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{i}}))\neq 0
and
q_{k}
a path fragment from
c_{k}
in
(C,\gamma)
, for
k\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
.

The set of all paths from
c\in C
in
(C,\gamma)
is denoted
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}
. We also write
({\mathsf{Paths}}_{C},\zeta_{C})
for the
C\times F
-subcoalgebra of
(Z_{C},\zeta_{C})
whose elements are paths from some state of
(C,\gamma)
. (That this is a subcoalgebra follows immediately from the definition of a path from
c\in C
.) For simplicity of notation, the dependency on
\gamma
is left implicit in both cases.

The difference between Definitions
2.2
and
2.2
is that the latter requires
non-zero
weights for all transitions belonging to paths/path fragments in
(C,\gamma)
. One consequence of this, which will be exploited later, is that there are only
finitely many
path fragments of a given depth from any state of
(C,\gamma)
.

In what follows, we will write
(c,i)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{i}})
for the path fragment with root
(c,i)
and immediate leaves given by
(c_{1},\iota_{1}(*)),\ldots,(c_{j_{i}},\iota_{1}(*))
, and
(c,i)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{i}})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{j_{i}}/c_{j_{i}}]
for the set of paths obtained by replacing each leaf node
(c_{i},\iota_{1}(*))
of
(c,i)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{i}})
by one of the paths in
A_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
. As a result, if any of the sets
A_{i}
is empty, then so is
(c,i)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{i}})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{j_{i}}/c_{j_{i}}]
.

2.3.
Semiring-Valued Relations and Relation Lifting

Throughout this section we fix a partial commutative semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
satisfying Assumption
2.1
.

We let
{\mathsf{Rel}}
denote the category
1
1

1

To keep notation simple, the dependency on
S
is left implicit.
with objects given by triples
(X,Y,R)
, where
R:X\times Y\to S
is an
S
-valued relation
, and with arrows from
(X,Y,R)
to
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},R^{\prime})
given by pairs of functions
(f,g)
as below, such that
R\sqsubseteq R^{\prime}\circ(f\times g)
:

\textstyle{X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sqsubseteq}
\scriptstyle{f\times g}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}

Here, the order
\sqsubseteq
on
S
has been extended pointwise to
S
-valued relations with the same carrier. We write
q:{\mathsf{Rel}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}\times{\mathsf{Set}}
for the functor taking
(X,Y,R)
to
(X,Y)
and
(f,g)
to itself. It follows easily that
q
is a fibration, with reindexing functors
(f,g)^{*}:{\mathsf{Rel}}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}\to{\mathsf{Rel}}_{X,Y}
taking
R^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\to S
to
R^{\prime}\circ(f\times g):X\times Y\to S
. We also write
{\mathsf{Rel}}_{X,Y}
for the
fibre over
(X,Y)
, i.e. the subcategory of
{\mathsf{Rel}}
with objects given by
S
-valued relations over
X\times Y
and arrows given by
({\mathsf{id}}_{X},{\mathsf{id}}_{Y})
.

{defi}

[Relation lifting]
An
S
-valued relation lifting
for a functor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is a
fibred
functor
2
2

2

That is, a functor which preserves reindexings.

L:{\mathsf{Rel}}\to{\mathsf{Rel}}
making the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Set}}\times{\mathsf{Set}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\times F}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Set}}\times{\mathsf{Set}}}

[
Cîr17
]
shows how to canonically lift polynomial endofunctors to the category of
S
-valued relations. The definition of the lifting makes use of the partial semiring structure on
S
.

{defi}

For a polynomial endofunctor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
, the
relation lifting

{\mathsf{Rel}}(F):{\mathsf{Rel}}\to{\mathsf{Rel}}
is defined by structural induction on
F
:

•

If
F={\mathsf{Id}}
,
{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
takes an
S
-valued relation to itself.

•

If
F=C
,
{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
takes an
S
-valued relation to the
equality relation

{\mathsf{Eq}}(C):C\times C\to S
given by

{\mathsf{Eq}}_{C}(c,c^{\prime})~=~\begin{cases}1,\text{ if }c=c^{\prime}\\
0,\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

•

If
F=F_{1}\times F_{2}
,
{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
takes an
S
-valued relation
R:X\times Y\to S
to:

\!\!{\small\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 337.70146pt\raise 4.50208pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50208pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bullet}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 347.51396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 347.51396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.}

The functoriality of this definition follows from the preservation of
\sqsubseteq
by
\bullet
.

•

if
F=\coprod_{i\in I}F_{i}
,
{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)(R):(\coprod_{i\in I}F_{i}X)\times(\coprod_{i\in I}F_{i}Y)\to S
is defined by case analysis:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)(R)(\iota_{i}(u),\iota_{j}(v))

\displaystyle~=~\begin{cases}{\mathsf{Rel}}(F_{i})(R)(u,v),&\text{ if }i=j\\
0,&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

for
i,j\in I
,
u\in F_{i}X
and
v\in F_{j}Y
.

It follows immediately from the above definition that
q\circ{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)=(F\times F)\circ q
. Moreover, an easy inductive proof shows that:

(1)

{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
is a fibred functor,

(2)

{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
preserves joins of increasing countable chains and meets of decreasing countable chains in each fibre of
q
.

Remark 3
.

When
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
,
S
-valued relations
R:X\times Y\to S
coincide with standard ones
R\subseteq X\times Y
. In this case, the notion of relation lifting of Definition
2.3
also coincides with the standard one, as described e.g. in
[
Jac16
]
[Chapter 3].

A special relation lifting called
extension lifting
is defined in
[
Cîr17
]
for any commutative, partially additive monad
{\mathsf{T}}
. The extension lifting
{\mathsf{E}}_{\mathsf{T}}:{\mathsf{Rel}}\to{\mathsf{Rel}}
lifts the endofunctor
{\mathsf{T}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
to
{\mathsf{Rel}}

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{E}}_{\mathsf{T}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Set}}\times{\mathsf{Set}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Set}}\times{\mathsf{Set}}}

in a canonical way. In the special case when
{\mathsf{T}}
is the partial semiring monad
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
, the extension lifting takes
R:X\times Y\to S
to the relation
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(R):{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(X)\times Y\to S
given by

{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(R)(\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i},y)=\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\bullet R(x_{i},y)

(2)

with
c_{i}\in S
and
x_{i}\in X
for
i\in I
, and
y\in Y
. (Note here that the definedness of
\sum_{i}c_{i}\bullet R(x_{i},y)
follows from the definedness of
\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}
by (
3
) of Remark
1
.) Fibredness of
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
also follows directly from its definition.

Remark 4
.

An alternative definition of the extension relation lifting, applicable to any strong monad
{\mathsf{T}}
, maps
R:X\times Y\to{\mathsf{T}}1
to

\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{st}}^{\prime}_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}(X\times Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}(R)}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}^{2}1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}1}

This general definition is canonical in the sense that the relation lifting of
R
is its unique extension to a left-linear map – note that both
{\mathsf{T}}X
and
{\mathsf{T}}1
are the carriers of free
{\mathsf{T}}
-algebras. It is straightforward to check that, for partial semiring monads, the two definitions coincide.

2.4.
Semiring-Valued Predicates and Predicate Lifting

The standard approach to defining the semantics of modal and fixpoint logics involves interpreting formulas as predicates over the state space of the system of interest. In the coalgebraic approach to modal logic, individual modal operators are interpreted using so called
predicate liftings

[
Pat03
]
. In order to follow the same approach for
quantitative
logics, we work with predicates valued in the partial commutative semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
used to model branching. A similar approach is taken in
[
SP11
]
, where
fuzzy predicate liftings
, valued in the unit interval, are used to provide a semantics to fuzzy description logics. The more general notion of predicate lifting considered here is also implicit in some of the earlier work on coalgebraic logic, e.g.
[
Kli05
,
Sch08
]
.

We let
{\mathsf{Pred}}
denote the category with objects given by pairs
(X,P)
with
P:X\to S
an
S
-valued predicate
, and arrows from
(X,P)
to
(X^{\prime},P^{\prime})
given by functions
f:X\to X^{\prime}
such that
P\sqsubseteq P^{\prime}\circ f
:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sqsubseteq}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}

As with
S
-valued relations, we obtain a fibration
p:{\mathsf{Pred}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
, with
p
taking
(X,P)
to
X
and
f
to itself. The fibre over
X
is denoted
{\mathsf{Pred}}_{X}
, and the reindexing functor
f^{*}:{\mathsf{Pred}}_{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathsf{Pred}}_{X}
takes
P^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S
to
P^{\prime}\circ f:X\to S
.

The next definition generalises
monotone
predicate liftings, as used in the semantics of coalgebraic modal logics
[
Pat03
]
, to a quantitative setting.

{defi}

[Predicate lifting]

An
(
S
-valued) predicate lifting
of arity
n\in\omega
for an endofunctor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is a fibred functor
L:{\mathsf{Pred}}^{n}\to{\mathsf{Pred}}
making the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Pred}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Pred}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Set}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Set}}

where the category
{\mathsf{Pred}}^{n}
has objects given by tuples
(X,P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
with
P_{i}:X\to S
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and arrows from
(X,P_{1},\ldots,P_{n})
to
(X^{\prime},P_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,P_{n}^{\prime})
given by functions
f:X\to X^{\prime}
such that
P_{i}\sqsubseteq P^{\prime}_{i}\circ f
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Remark 5
.

It is not difficult to see that predicate liftings as defined above coincide with
monotone
predicate liftings in the sense of
[
Pat03
]
, suitably generalised to an
S
-valued setting, that is, with monotone natural transformations
l:(S^{{}^{\_}})^{n}\Longrightarrow S^{{}^{\_}}\circ F
, where
S^{{}^{\_}}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is the contravariant functor mapping
X
to the set of
S
-valued functions on
X
. Here, a natural transformation
l
as above is
monotone
if whenever
f_{i}\sqsubseteq g_{i}
with
f_{i},g_{i}:X\to S
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, then also
l(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\sqsubseteq l(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
. The naturality of standard predicate liftings is here captured by the fibredness requirement on
{\mathsf{L}}
, whereas monotonicity corresponds to the functoriality of
{\mathsf{L}}
.

We now restrict attention to polynomial functors
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
, and show how to define a canonical
set
of predicate liftings for
F
by induction on its structure. The next definition exploits the observation that any polynomial endofunctor is naturally isomorphic to a coproduct of finite (including empty) products of identity functors.

{defi}

Let
F=\coprod_{i\in I}{\mathsf{Id}}^{j_{i}}
, with
j_{i}\in\omega
for
i\in I
. The set of predicate liftings
\Lambda=\{L_{i}\mid i\in I\}
has elements
L_{i}:{\mathsf{Pred}}^{j_{i}}\to{\mathsf{Pred}}
with
i\in I
given by:

(L_{i})_{X}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{j_{i}})(f)~=~\begin{cases}P_{1}(x_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet P_{j_{i}}(x_{j_{i}}),&\text{ if }f=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j_{i}})\in\iota_{i}({\mathsf{Id}}^{j_{i}})\\
0&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

The functoriality of this definition follows from the preservation of
\sqsubseteq
by
\bullet
. The fact that each
L_{i}
is a fibred functor follows directly from its definition. As a result of Assumption
2.1
, all these predicate liftings preserve joins of increasing countable chains and meets of decreasing countable chains in each argument.

Remark 6
.

The predicate liftings
L_{i}
of Definition
2.4
preserve joins of increasing countable chains as well as meets of decreasing countable chains in each fibre of
p
, in each argument. This is immediate from the definition of
L_{i}
and the preservation of such joins and meets by
\bullet
in each argument.

Remark 7
.

When
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the predicate liftings of Definition
2.4
are essentially the same as the
Nabla modality
of
[
Mos99
]
.

{exa}

For
F=\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}\,\simeq\,\{*\}+\coprod_{a\in A}{\mathsf{Id}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
, states in
F
-coalgebras unfold to (potentially infinite) binary trees with internal nodes labelled by elements of
A
and leaves not carrying any label. Definition
2.4
yields a nullary predicate lifting
L_{0}
and an
A
-indexed set of binary predicate liftings
(L_{a})_{a\in A}
:

\displaystyle(L_{0})_{X}(f)

\displaystyle~=~\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if }f=\iota_{1}(*)\\
0,&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases},

\displaystyle(L_{a})_{X}(P_{1},P_{2})(f)

\displaystyle~=~\begin{cases}P_{1}(x_{1})\bullet P_{2}(x_{2}),&\text{ if }f=\iota_{a}(x_{1},x_{2})\\
0,&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

As we are interested in
linear-time
logics, a special
extension lifting
, akin to the extension relation lifting of Section
2.3
, will be used to abstract away branching.

{defi}

Let
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
be a partial commutative semiring with associated monad
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
. The
extension predicate lifting

{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}:{\mathsf{Pred}}\to{\mathsf{Pred}}
is the lifting of
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
to
{\mathsf{Pred}}

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Pred}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Pred}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Set}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Set}}

which takes
P:X\to S
to the predicate
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(P):{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X\to S
given by

\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i}~\mapsto~\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\bullet P(x_{i})

with
c_{i}\in S
for
i\in I
being such that
\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}
is defined, and with
x_{i}\in X
for
i\in I
.

It then follows directly from the definition that
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
is a fibred functor. It also follows from Assumption
2.1
together with (
4
) and (
5
) of Remark
1
that
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
preserves the joins of increasing chains
P_{0}\sqsubseteq P_{1}\sqsubseteq\ldots
and the meets of decreasing chains
P_{0}\sqsupseteq P_{1}\sqsupseteq\ldots
in each fibre of
p
.

{exa}

For
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the predicate lifting
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
takes a standard predicate
P\subseteq X
to the predicate
\{Y\subseteq X\mid Y\text{ finite}\,,\,Y\cap P\neq\emptyset\}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}_{\omega}}X
, where
{\mathcal{P}_{\omega}}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is the finite powerset functor (naturally isomorphic to
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
). This corresponds to the standard
\Diamond
modality. For
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
, the predicate lifting
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
takes a predicate
P:X\to[0,1]
to the predicate
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(P):{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X\to[0,1]
given by
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(P)(\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in I}(c_{i}*P(x_{i}))
. For
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
, the predicate lifting
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
takes a predicate
P:X\to\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
to the predicate
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(P):{\mathsf{T}}_{S}X\to\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
given by
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(P)(\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i})=\min\limits_{i\in I}(c_{i}+P(x_{i}))
.

Remark 8
.

{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(P)
can alternatively be defined as
\mu_{1}\circ{\mathsf{T}}_{S}P
, for
P:X\to S
.

2.5.
Finite and Maximal Traces via Relation Lifting

We now summarise the definitions of
finite trace behaviour
and
maximal trace behaviour
of a state in a coalgebra with branching, as defined in
[
Cîr17
]
. The approach in loc. cit. applies to coalgebras of functors obtained as compositions of a single partially additive monad and a finite number of polynomial endofunctors on
{\mathsf{Set}}
. For simplicity, here we restrict attention to compositions of type
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
, with
S
a partial commutative semiring and
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
a polynomial functor.

The notion of
coalgebraic bisimulation
provides a canonical and uniform observational equivalence relation between states of
F
-coalgebras. One of the many (and largely equivalent) definitions of bisimulation involves lifting the functor
F
to the category of two-valued relations (obtained in our setting by taking
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
). Then,
F
-bisimilarity
, defined as the largest bisimulation between the states of two
F
-coalgebras, can be characterised as the greatest fixpoint of a monotone operator on the complete lattice of relations between the underlying state spaces
[
Jac16
]
. A similar approach is taken in
[
Cîr17
]
to define the
extent
to which a state in a coalgebra with branching can exhibit a given maximal/finite trace. The definition in loc. cit. differs from the above characterisation of bisimilarity in two ways: (i)
S
-valued relations are used in place of two-valued relations, and (ii) the relation lifting employed also involves the extension relation lifting
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
defined earlier, as the goal is to relate
branching-time
behaviours with
linear-time
ones, as opposed to relating behaviours of the same coalgebraic type.

{defiC}

[
[
Cîr17
]
]

Let
(Z,\zeta)
denote the final
F
-coalgebra. The
maximal trace behaviour
of a state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is the greatest fixpoint
{\mathsf{tr}}_{\gamma}:C\times Z\to S
of the monotone operator
{\mathsf{O}}:{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,Z}\to{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,Z}
given by the composition

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{FC,FZ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC,FZ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma\times\zeta)^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,Z}}

(3)

Two states
c,d\in C
are said to be
maximal trace equivalent
, written
c\simeq_{\mathsf{tr}}d
, iff
{\mathsf{tr}}_{\gamma}(c,z)={\mathsf{tr}}_{\gamma}(d,z)
for all
z\in Z
.

We spell out the definition of the operator
{\mathsf{O}}
in Definition
2.5
, based on the definitions of
{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
(Definition
2.3
) and
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
(see (
2
)). For an
S
-valued relation
R:C\times Z\to S
and
(c,z)\in C\times Z
with
\gamma(c)=\sum\limits_{i\in I}s_{i}\iota_{\lambda_{i}}(c_{1}^{i},\ldots,c_{j_{i}}^{i})
and
\zeta(z)=\iota_{\lambda}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
, we have:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{O}}(R)(c,z)=\sum\limits_{i\in I\,\text{s.t.}\,\lambda_{i}=\lambda}s_{i}\bullet R(c^{i}_{1},z_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet R(c^{i}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)},z_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\,.

Thus, if the trace
z
prescribes
\lambda
as the next transition and then continues with traces
z_{1},\ldots,z_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
, then only
\lambda
-transitions from
c
are taken into account when defining
{\mathsf{O}}(R)(c,z)
. This value is obtained by summing, across all
\lambda
-transitions from
c
(
\lambda_{i}=\lambda
), the values
R(c^{i}_{1},z_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet R(c^{i}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)},z_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
weighted with the corresponding transition weights (
s_{i}
).

The existence of a greatest fixpoint for
{\mathsf{O}}
follows from the Knaster-Tarski theorem
[
Tar55
]
. This result will be used repeatedly in what follows.

{thmC}

[
[
Tar55
]
]

Let
{\mathsf{O}}:L\to L
be a monotone operator on a complete lattice
(L,\sqsubseteq)
. Then, the set of fixpoints of
{\mathsf{O}}
forms a complete lattice, also under
\sqsubseteq
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.5
, the following result provides a way to obtain the least and greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
.

{thmC}

[
[
CC79
]
]

Let
{\mathsf{O}}:L\to L
be a monotone operator on a complete lattice
(L,\sqsubseteq)
. Consider the ascending chain
({\mathsf{O}}^{\alpha}(\bot))_{\alpha}
, with
\alpha
ranging over the ordinals, defined by
{\mathsf{O}}^{0}(\bot)=\bot
,
{\mathsf{O}}^{\alpha+1}(\bot)={\mathsf{O}}({\mathsf{O}}^{\alpha}(\bot))
for any ordinal
\alpha
, and
{\mathsf{O}}^{\alpha}(\bot)=\bigsqcup_{\beta<\alpha}{\mathsf{O}}^{\beta}(\bot)
for
\alpha
a limit ordinal. Then, the least fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
is given by
{\mathsf{O}}^{\gamma}(\bot)
for some ordinal
\gamma
. The greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
is obtained dually, via a descending chain
({\mathsf{O}}^{\alpha}(\top))_{\alpha}
.

The fact that the operator
{\mathsf{O}}
of Definition
2.5
is monotone follows from the functoriality of each of
{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)
,
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
and
(\gamma\times\zeta)^{*}
.

The definition of
finite
trace behaviour simply replaces maximal traces by finite ones.

{defiC}

[
[
Cîr17
]
]

Let
(I,\iota)
denote the initial
F
-algebra. The
finite trace behaviour
of a state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is the greatest fixpoint of the operator
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}:{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,I}\to{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,I}
given by the composition

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{FC,FI}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC,FI}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma\times\iota^{-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,I}}

(4)

The operator
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
is thus similar to the operator
{\mathsf{O}}
, except that the final
F
-coalgebra is replaced by the initial
F
-algebra. We note that taking the
least
fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
would yield an equivalent definition, since in this case the fixpoint is unique. Informally, this is because the elements of
I
are
finite
traces, and because the
n
th approximations
({\mathsf{O}}^{\prime})^{n}(\bot)
and
({\mathsf{O}}^{\prime})^{n}(\top)
of the least, respectively greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
will coincide on pairs
(c,i)\in C\times I
with
i
a finite trace of depth at most
n
, for all
n\in\omega
(and thus so will the least and greatest fixpoints of
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
).

{exa}

(1)

For
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
/
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
relates a state
c
in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
with a maximal/finite trace
t
iff there exists a sequence of choices in the unfolding of
\gamma
starting from
c
that results in an
F
-behaviour bisimilar to
t
.

(2)

For
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
, the greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
/
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
yields, for each state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra and each maximal/finite trace, the accumulated probability (across all branches) of this trace being exhibited. Here we note that, for
infinite
maximal traces, the associated probability is often
0
. Arguably, this has limited usefulness, and a measure-theoretic definition that takes into account the accumulated probabilities of exhibiting
finite prefixes
of infinite traces would in this case be more useful. The logics defined later in this paper do not suffer from this issue, as they allow expressing the probability of exhibiting certain
sets
of traces, including traces with a given finite prefix.

(3)

For
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
, the greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
/
{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}
maps a pair
(c,t)
, with
c
a state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra and
t
a maximal/finite trace, to the minimal cost of exhibiting
t
from
c
. Intuitively, this is computed by adding the weights of individual transitions along the same branch, and minimising this sum across all the branches. Again, such minimal costs are often infinite for
infinite
maximal traces, but in this case this simply reflects the fact that infinitely running systems have infinite costs. While resource gain could be modelled by considering coalgebras of a different type (namely
S\times({\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F)
, with
F
as before and with the first compoment being used to associate resource gains to coalgebra
states
), we leave a detailed study of this more general case to future work.

2.6.
\mu
- and
\nu
-Extents via Predicate Lifting

We now define the notions of
\nu
-extent
and
\mu
-extent
of a coalgebra with branching, which generalise the non-emptiness of the set of maximal, respectively finite traces in a system with non-deterministic branching, to systems with quantitative branching. These notions were introduced in
[
CSH17
]
in order to provide automata-based model checking techniques for the logics in
[
Cîr14
,
Cîr15
]
. Here, the notion of
\nu
-extent will be key to defining a notion of linear-time behaviour of a state in a system with branching (Section
7
), as well as a path-based semantics for quantitative, linear-time fixpoint logics interpreted over such systems (Section
5
).

{defiC}

[
[
CSH17
]
]

The
\nu
-extent
(
\mu
-extent
) of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
is the greatest fixpoint (resp. least fixpoint) of the operator on
S^{C}
taking
p:C\to S
to the composition

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}Fp}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(\bullet_{F})}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}S={\mathsf{T}}_{S}^{2}1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}1=S}

where
\bullet_{F}:FS\to S
is given by
\bullet_{F}(\iota_{i}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{j_{i}}))=s_{1}\bullet\ldots\bullet s_{j_{i}}
for
i\in I
. We write
{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}:C\to S
for the
\nu
-extent of
(C,\gamma)
.

The above operator uses a one-step unfolding of the coalgebra structure to compute (a finer approximation of) the extent of a state based on (current approximations of) the extents of its immediate successors. As the generality of
F
allows for immediate successors which are
tuples
of states, the semiring multiplication also needs to be used (namely in
\bullet_{F}
). On the other hand, the monad multiplication is used to accumulate the values from different branches. The composition in Definition
2.6
takes
c\in C
with
\gamma(c)=\sum_{i}s_{i}(c_{i}^{1},\ldots,c_{i}^{j_{i}})
to
\mu_{1}(\sum_{i}s_{i}(p(c_{i}^{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet p(c_{i}^{j_{i}})))
. As a result, the extent in state
c
is a sum, across all transitions from
c
, of the extents of the successors of
c
, scaled by the weights of the corresponding transitions. In case of transitions with
several
successor states, the extents of these states are first multiplied and then scaled by the transition weight.

{exa}

(1)

For
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the
\nu
- (resp.
\mu
-)extent has a value of
1
on a state iff there exists a maximal (resp. finite) trace from that state, arising from a sequence of choices in the branching behaviour. (Such a trace will not exist e.g. from a state which offers no choices for proceeding.)

(2)

For
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
, the
\nu
- (resp.
\mu
-)extent on a state gives the probability of not deadlocking (resp. not deadlocking and not executing forever) from that state.

(3)

For
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
, the
\nu
- (resp.
\mu
-)extent on a given state gives the minimal cost that can be achieved along a maximal (resp. finite) trace from that state.

Example 9

(
[
CSH17
]
)
.

Consider the
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras below, with
F=\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
and
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
(resp.
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
), and note that in both cases, all finite traces must end with a transition from
y
to
\iota_{1}(*)
. The
\nu
-extent maps
x
to
\frac{2}{5}
,
y
to
\frac{3}{5}
and
z
to
\frac{1}{5}
(resp.
x
and
y
to
1
and
z
to
0
), whereas the
\mu
-extent again maps
x
to
\frac{2}{5}
,
y
to
\frac{3}{5}
and
z
to
\frac{1}{5}
(resp.
x
and
z
to
4
and
y
to
2
). Intuitively, the reason for the
\mu
- and
\nu
-extents being the same in the probabilistic case is that the probability of never reaching
y
from either
x
or
z
is
0
.

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2},a}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2},b}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2},*}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{4},c}
\textstyle{z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2},c}
\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{4},c}

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2,a}
\scriptstyle{1,b}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2,*}
\scriptstyle{0,c}
\textstyle{z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0,c}
\scriptstyle{0,c}

To see why the
\nu
-extents are as claimed in the probabilistic case, note that they are the greatest (and in this case unique) solution of the following system of equations:

\displaystyle\qquad\begin{bmatrix}x&=&\frac{1}{2}*y+\frac{1}{2}*z\\
y&=&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}*x\\
z&=&\frac{1}{4}*x+\frac{1}{2}*z\end{bmatrix}

It turns out that the notions of
\nu
- and
\mu
-extent can be used to give alternative characterisations of the notions of maximal trace behaviour, and respectively of finite trace behaviour. Specifically, these can be shown to coincide with the
\nu
-, respectively
\mu
-extents of certain ”products” of the coalgebra in question with the final, respectively the initial
F
-algebra, where the latter two are now viewed as
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras.

{defiC}

[
[
CSH17
]
]

The
product
of
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras
(C,\gamma)
and
(D,\delta)
is the
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra with carrier
C\times D
and transition function
\gamma\otimes\delta
given by

\textstyle{C\times D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma\times\delta}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC\times{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FD\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{dst}}_{FC,FD}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(FC\times FD)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle F\pi_{1},F\pi_{2}\rangle^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}F(C\times D)}

where
{\mathsf{dst}}
is the double strength of the monad
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
(see Section
2.1
) and
\langle F\pi_{1},F\pi_{2}\rangle^{*}
is pre-composition with
\langle F\pi_{1},F\pi_{2}\rangle:F(C\times D)\to FC\times FD
.

The effect of pre-composing with
\langle F\pi_{1},F\pi_{2}\rangle
is that pairs of non-matching one-step behaviours are discarded from the resulting coalgebra. Thus, the product coalgebra collects the common
F
-behaviour of the two coalgebras, suitably weighted according to the weights of the two coalgebras.

The following result, while new, resembles
[
CSH17
, Theorem 14]
in both its statement and its proof.

Theorem 10
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra, let
(Z,\zeta)
denote the final
F
-coalgebra, and let
(I,\iota)
denote the initial
F
-coalgebra. The following hold:

(1)

Both the
\mu
-extent and the
\nu
-extent of the product coalgebra
(C\times I,\gamma\otimes\iota)
coincide with the finite trace behaviour
{\mathsf{ftr}}_{\gamma}:C\times I\to S
.

(2)

The
\nu
-extent of the product coalgebra
(C\times Z,\gamma\otimes\zeta)
coincides with the maximal trace behaviour
{\mathsf{tr}}_{\gamma}:C\times Z\to S
.

Proof 2.1

(
Proof (sketch).).

The proof is similar to that of
[
CSH17
, Theorem 14]
and involves showing that the operators used in the definition of the maximal/finite trace behaviour on the one hand, and of the
\mu
-/
\nu
-extent of the product automaton on the other, coincide.

2.7.
Coalgebraic Fixpoint Logics

We now recall how a
two-valued
fixpoint logic for
F
-coalgebras can be defined from a set of monotone predicate liftings for
F

[
CKP11
]
. A fragment of this logic, which does not contain conjunctions and arbitrary disjunctions, will later (Section
3
) also be given a quantitative interpretation over
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, with
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
a polynomial functor and
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
as before. Given a functor
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
, a (
\{0,1\}
-valued) fixpoint logic for
F
-coalgebras is parameterised by a set of
\{0,1\}
-valued predicate liftings for
F
(see Definition
2.4
). Concretely, given a set
\Lambda
of modal operators with associated
\{0,1\}
-valued, monotone predicate liftings
(\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket)_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
for
F
, the associated fixpoint logic has syntax:

\displaystyle\textstyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}\ni\varphi::=\bot\,\mid\,\top\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\,\mid\,\varphi\vee\psi\,\mid\,\varphi\wedge\psi\,\mid\,\mu x.\varphi\,\mid\,\nu x.\varphi

with
x\in\mathcal{V}
for some set
\mathcal{V}
of variables. Here,
{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)
is the arity of the modal operator
\langle\lambda\rangle
. Then, for an
F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
and a
valuation

V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{C}
, the semantics
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}\in\{0,1\}^{C}
of formulas
\varphi
as above is given by:

•

\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)=0
for
c\in C
,

•

\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)=1
for
c\in C
,

•

\llbracket x\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}=V(x)
,

•

\llbracket\varphi\vee\psi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)\vee\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)
for
c\in C
,

•

\llbracket\varphi\wedge\psi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)\wedge\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)
for
c\in C
,

•

\llbracket\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}=\gamma^{*}(\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket_{C}(\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V},\ldots,\llbracket\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}))
, where
\gamma^{*}:\{0,1\}^{FC}\to\{0,1\}^{C}
performs reindexing along
\gamma:C\to FC
.

•

\llbracket\mu x.\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V\setminus\{x\}}
(
\llbracket\nu x.\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V\setminus\{x\}}
) is the least (resp. greatest) fixpoint of the operator
{\mathsf{O}}_{\varphi}
on
\{0,1\}^{C}
taking
p:C\to\{0,1\}
to
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V[p/x]}
, where the valuation
V[p/x]:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{C}
takes
x
to
p
and
y\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{x\}
to
V(y)
.

The existence of the fixpoints required to interpret fixpoint formulas
\mu x.\varphi
and
\nu x.\varphi
is guaranteed by Theorem
2.5
, given the complete lattice structure of
\{0,1\}^{C}
(inherited from
\{0,1\}
) and the monotonicity of the operators involved. The latter is an immediate consequence of the monotonicity of the predicate liftings.

Remark 11
.

One can take
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
to be a polynomial functor and the predicate liftings
\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket
to be as in Definition
2.4
(with
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
). As these predicate liftings preserve joins of increasing countable chains and meets of decreasing countable chains in
{\mathsf{Pred}}_{C}
(see Remark
6
), the operator
{\mathsf{O}}_{\varphi}
used in defining the semantics of fixpoint formulas is both continuous and co-continuous. It then follows by Kleene’s fixpoint theorem (included below for reference) that the least (respectively greatest) fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}_{\varphi}
can be constructed as the join of the increasing chain
0\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{O}}_{\varphi}(0)\sqsubseteq\ldots
(respectively the meet of the decreasing chain
1\sqsupseteq{\mathsf{O}}_{\varphi}(1)\sqsupseteq\ldots
) in
{\mathsf{Pred}}_{C}
.

{thmC}

[
[
Kle52
]
]
Let
{\mathsf{O}}:L\to L
be a continuous operator on a complete lattice
(L,\sqsubseteq)
. Then, the least fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}
is given by
\bigsqcup_{\alpha<\omega}{\mathsf{O}}^{\alpha}(\bot)
.

3.
Quantitative Linear-Time Fixpoint Logics

We are now ready to define
linear-time
fixpoint logics for coalgebras of type
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
, where
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is the monad induced by a partial semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
whose associated order
\sqsubseteq
satisfies Assumption
2.1
, and
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is a polynomial functor. Similarly to Definition
2.4
, we assume that
F
is given by
\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}{\mathsf{Id}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
, with
\Lambda
a set and each
\lambda\in\Lambda
being assigned a finite arity
{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)
. Our logics will be valued into the semiring carrier
S
, and will use modalities from
\Lambda
with associated
S
-valued
predicate liftings
(\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket)_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
for
F
as in Definition
2.4
. A step-wise semantics for these logics is given herewith, while an alternative, path-based semantics is described in Section
5
and proved equivalent to the step-wise semantics in Section
6
.

{defi}

[Linear-time fixpoint logic
[
Cîr14
]
]

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a set (of variables). The logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
has syntax given by

\displaystyle\textstyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}\ni\varphi::=~\bot\,\mid\,\top\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\,\mid\,\mu x.\varphi\,\mid\,\nu x.\varphi

with
x\in\mathcal{V}
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
. We write
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
for the set of
closed
formulas (containing no free variables).

While here we are concerned with a
quantitative
interpretation of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
over
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, we immediately note that
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
also has a
qualitative
interpretation over
F
-coalgebras, described in Section
2.7
. For this reason, we can view
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formulas as describing
linear-time
properties, that is,
qualitative
properties of
F
-structures.

A step-wise semantics for
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
is defined below, by induction on the structure of formulas.

{defi}

[Step-wise semantics for
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
]

For a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
and a valuation
V:\mathcal{V}\to S^{C}
(interpreting the variables in
\mathcal{V}
as
S
-valued predicates over
C
), the denotation
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}\in S^{C}
of a formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
is defined inductively on the structure of
\varphi
by

•

\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}
is given by
\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}(c)=0
for all
c\in C
,

•

\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}
is given by the
\nu
-extent
{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}:C\to S
,

•

\llbracket x\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}=V(x)
,

•

\llbracket\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}=\gamma^{*}({\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}(\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket_{C}(\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V},\ldots,\llbracket\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V})))
, where
\gamma^{*}:S^{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC}\to S^{C}
denotes reindexing along
\gamma:C\to{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC
.

•

\llbracket\mu x.\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V\setminus\{x\}}
(
\llbracket\nu x.\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V\setminus\{x\}}
) is the least (resp. greatest) fixpoint of the operator on
S^{C}
taking
p\in S^{C}
to
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V[p/x]}
, where the valuation
V[p/x]:\mathcal{V}\to S^{C}
is defined as in Section
2.7
.

(In the third clause, both the formal sum notation and the action of the monad multiplication have been extended pointwise to functions on
C
.)

The fact that the operator used to interpret fixpoint formulas is monotone follows from the functoriality of both
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
and the predicate liftings
\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket
with
\lambda\in\Lambda
. The existence of the required least and greatest fixpoints now follows from Theorem
2.5
.

The semantics of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
thus resembles that of coalgebraic fixpoint logics (see Section
2.7
), with the following differences: (i) the interpretation of a formula is an
S
-valued
predicate over the state space, (ii) the
extension
predicate lifting is used alongside the predicate liftings
\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket
to give semantics to modal formulas, and (iii) the constant
\top
has a
coinductive
interpretation. In particular, the use of the extension predicate lifting to abstract away branching behaviour is what makes
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
a
linear-time logic
.

We argue that a coinductive interpretation of truth is a natural generalisation of the semantics of (the existential variant of) the linear-time
\mu
-calculus, wherein a state satisfies a fixpoint formula if and only if there exists an infinite path from that state which satisfies the formula. In particular, a state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra from which
no
maximal path exists interprets
\top
as
0
, whereas the interpretation of
\top
in a state which admits one or more maximal paths ”measures” the set of
all
such maximal paths, as a value in
S
which is computed coinductively. It is precisely this coinductive interpretation of
\top
that allows the existence of an equivalent path-based semantics.

Remark 12
.

A restricted form of disjunction can easily be incorporated into the logics. Specifically, for each
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
with
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
, one can define a new modal operator
\langle\lambda\rangle\_\sqcup\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle\_
, of arity equal to
{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)+{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})
, with associated predicate lifting
L:{\mathsf{Pred}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)+{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})}\to{\mathsf{Pred}}
given by:

\displaystyle L_{X}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)},p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})}^{\prime})(f)

\displaystyle\!=\!\begin{cases}p_{1}(x_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet p_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}(x_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}),&\!\!\!\text{if }f=\iota_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{{\mathsf{ar}}{(\lambda)}})\\
p_{1}^{\prime}(y_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet p^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})}(y_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})}),&\!\!\!\text{if }f=\iota_{\lambda^{\prime}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{{\mathsf{ar}}{(\lambda^{\prime})}})\\
0,&\!\!\!\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\!.

Thus, the formula
\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\sqcup\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle(\varphi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\varphi^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})})
represents a disjunction which can be resolved using a one-step unfolding of the coalgebra structure (since each branch resulting from a one-step unfolding will match either
\langle\lambda\rangle
or
\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle
or none of them). Furthermore, the definition of such
guarded disjunctions
generalises to any choice of a finite subset of
\Lambda
. The qualitative interpretation of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
over
F
-coalgebras also extends naturally to such guarded disjunctions. Then, in the presence of such enhanced modalities,
deterministic
parity automata over
F
-structures
3
3

3

This includes automata over infinite words (
F=A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
) and also over infinite trees (
F=A\times{\mathsf{Id}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
).
can be encoded as formulas of our logic. The reader is referred to
[
CSH17
]
, wherein translations from
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formulas to
parity
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-automata
(
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras equipped with a parity map) and back are described. It is worth noting that the syntax of the logics in loc. cit. includes weighted sums; in the absence of weighted sums, the resulting automata are essentially deterministic ones
4
4

4

The resulting automata have
at most
one transition labelled by any given
\lambda\in\Lambda
from each state.
.

{asm}

In what follows, we assume that the additional modal operators described in Remark
12
are present in the logics. With this extended syntax, the
qualitative
semantics of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
(given by a variant of the semantics in Section
2.7
which leaves out the cases for disjunctions and conjunctions of formulas) extends to the additional modal operators
\langle\lambda\rangle\_\sqcup\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle\_
, via predicate liftings whose components
L_{X}:({\mathcal{P}}(X))^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)+{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})}\to{\mathcal{P}}(FX)
are given by

\displaystyle L_{X}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)},Y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,Y_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})}^{\prime})~=~

\displaystyle\{\iota_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{{\mathsf{ar}}{(\lambda)}})\mid x_{i}\in Y_{i}\text{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}\,\}\,\cup

\displaystyle\{\iota_{\lambda^{\prime}}(x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{ar}}{(\lambda^{\prime})}})\mid x_{i}^{\prime}\in Y_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda^{\prime})\}\,\}\,.

(In the above,
\{0,1\}
-valued predicates on
X
are identified with elements of
{\mathcal{P}}(X)
.) In other words, under the qualitative semantics, the formula
\langle\lambda\rangle\varphi\sqcup\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle\psi
with
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
is equivalent to the disjunction
\langle\lambda\rangle\varphi\vee\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle\psi
.

{exa}

This example describes instantiations to concrete semirings of our quantitative logics. The characterisations provided below are a direct consequence of our main result (Theorem
34
), and at this point only serve as intuitions for the semantics.

(1)

As noted in Remark
7
, when
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the predicate liftings
\llbracket\lambda\rrbracket
with
\lambda\in\Lambda
are closely related to the Nabla modality of coalgebraic logics for
F
-coalgebras
[
Mos99
]
. However, the use of the extension predicate lifting
{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}
to abstract away branching results in the logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
being similar to (the existential variant of) the
linear-time

\mu
