-calculus: a
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formula holds in a state
s
of a coalgebra with non-deterministic branching whenever a maximal trace (element of the final
F
-coalgebra) satisfying that formula in the qualitative sense can be exhibited from
s
. Yet, unlike the linear-time
\mu
-calculus, which has a path-based semantics and contains conjunction operators, our logics have a step-wise semantics which prevents conjunctions with the expected interpretation from being included.

(2)

For
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
,
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formulas measure the likelihood of satisfying a certain linear-time property. In spite of the absence of disjunctions or conjunctions from the logics, if one additionally assumes that
F
specifies word-like behaviour (that is,
F=\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}{\mathsf{Id}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
, with
{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\in\{0,1\}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
), our logics have the same expressive power, when interpreted qualitatively over
F
-structures, as deterministic parity automata (see
[
CSH17
]
for details). Thus, our quantitative logics match the expressive power of logics such as LTL or the linear-time
\mu
-calculus, when interpreted over
probabilistic
models.

(3)

For
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
or one of its bounded variants,
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formulas measure the minimal cost needed to satisfy a certain linear-time property.

{exa}

To illustrate the use of modalities incorporating guarded disjunctions, first let
F=A\times{\mathsf{Id}}\simeq\coprod\limits_{a\in A}{\mathsf{Id}}
with
A
finite, and define
\langle\overline{a}\rangle\varphi::=\bigsqcup_{b\in A\setminus\{a\}}\langle b\rangle\varphi
for
a\in A
. Then, the
qualitative
properties stating that
a\in A
appears (i) always, (ii) eventually, (iii) finitely often, and (iv) infinitely often in the unfolding of a state in an
F
-coalgebra are captured by the formulas (i)
\nu x.\langle a\rangle x
, (ii)
\mu x.(\langle a\rangle\top\sqcup\langle\overline{a}\rangle x)
, (iii)
\mu x.\nu y.(\langle a\rangle x\sqcup\langle\overline{a}\rangle y)
, and (iv)
\nu x.\mu y.(\langle a\rangle x\sqcup\langle\overline{a}\rangle y)
, respectively. Now let
F=\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}\simeq\{*\}+\coprod\limits_{a\in A}{\mathsf{Id}}
with
A
finite, and define
\langle A\rangle\varphi::=\bigsqcup_{a\in A}\langle a\rangle\varphi
. Then, the formula
\mu x.(\langle*\rangle\sqcup\langle A\rangle x)
captures terminating behaviour. Finally, let
F=\{a,b\}\times{\mathsf{Id}}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
. In this case, the behaviour of states in
F
-coalgebras is
tree-shaped
. Then, the formula
\mu x.(\langle a\rangle(\top,\top)\sqcup\langle b\rangle(x,x))
captures the qualitative property that
a
occurs eventually on
every
branch of the unfolding of a state in an
F
-coalgebra. (Note that the property that
a
occurs eventually on
some
branch of the unfolding of a state in an
F
-coalgebra is
not
expressible – a non-guarded disjunction would be needed to make a choice between the left and the right branch.) All the above formulas can also be interpreted over coalgebras with non-deterministic, probabilistic or weighted branching, with the resulting semantics measuring the extent (possibility, likelihood and minimal cost, respectively) of exhibiting the corresponding qualitative property.

{exa}

Assume
F=\{*\}+A\times{\mathsf{Id}}
and consider the formula
\mu x.(\langle a\rangle\top\sqcup\langle\overline{a}\rangle x)
, expressing the qualitative property ”eventually
a
” (where
\langle\overline{a}\rangle
is defined as in Example
3
). Its semantics in the two coalgebras from Example
9
is given by
(x\mapsto\frac{2}{5},y\mapsto\frac{1}{10},z\mapsto\frac{1}{5})
and respectively
(x\mapsto 4,y\mapsto 4,z\mapsto 4)
:

(1)

For the first coalgebra, using the fact that the
\nu
-extent of state
y
is
\frac{3}{5}
, it follows that
\llbracket\langle a\rangle\top\rrbracket
is given by
(x\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{3}{5}=\frac{3}{10},y\mapsto 0,z\mapsto 0)
. Then,
\llbracket\mu x.(\langle a\rangle\top\sqcup\langle\overline{a}\rangle x)\rrbracket
is obtained as the least solution of the following system of equations:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x=\frac{3}{10}+\frac{1}{2}z\\
y=\frac{1}{4}x\\
z=\frac{1}{4}x+\frac{1}{2}z\end{cases}

(2)

In the second coalgebra,
\llbracket\langle a\rangle\top\rrbracket
is given by
(x\mapsto 4,y\mapsto 0,z\mapsto 0)
. Then,
\llbracket\mu x.(\langle a\rangle\top\sqcup\langle\overline{a}\rangle x)\rrbracket
is the least (w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq
, which is now
\geq
on
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
) solution of:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x=\min(4,1+z)\\
y=x\\
z=\min(x,z)\end{cases}

We conclude this section by recalling an alternative, automata-theoretic characterisation of the semantics of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, given in
[
CSH17
]
, which will prove useful later on. A notion of
quantitative parity automaton
is defined in
[
CSH17
]
as a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(A,\alpha)
together with a function
\Omega:A\to\{1,2,\ldots\}
with finite range, called a
parity map
. A translation from
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formulas to such parity automata is then provided in loc. cit., and an automata-theoretic characterisation of the semantics of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
is also given. This characterisation makes use of a generalisation of the notions of
\mu
-/
\nu
-extent of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra, defined as a
nested
fixpoint which takes into account the parity map of a quantitative parity automaton. As is the case for existential LTL/the existential variant of the linear-time
\mu
-calculus, the semantics of a
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
-formula in a given coalgebra is recovered as the (generalised) extent of a parity automaton obtained as the product (Definition
2.6
) between the coalgebra in question and the automaton induced by the formula, with parities inherited from the automaton.

{defiC}

[
[
CSH17
]
]

Let
(A,\alpha,\Omega)
be a quantitative parity automaton with
{\mathsf{ran}}(\Omega)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
, let
A_{k}=\{a\in A\mid\Omega(a)=k\}
, and let
\alpha_{k}=\alpha\circ\iota_{k}:A_{k}\to{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FA
denote the restriction of
\alpha
to
A_{k}
. The
extent
{\mathsf{e}}_{\alpha}=[{\mathsf{e}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{e}}_{n}]:A\to S
of
(A,\alpha,\Omega)
is the solution of the nested equational system
5
5

5

See e.g.
[
AN01
]
for a definition of nested equational systems and their solutions.

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}&=_{\mu}&\mu_{1}\circ{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(\bullet_{F})\circ T_{S}F[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]\circ\alpha_{1}\\
&\vdots\\
u_{n}&=_{\eta}&\mu_{1}\circ{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(\bullet_{F})\circ T_{S}F[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]\circ\alpha_{n}\end{bmatrix}

(5)

with
\eta=\mu
(
\eta=\nu
) if
n
is odd (resp. even), with variables
u_{k}
ranging over the lattice
(S^{A_{k}},\sqsubseteq)
(and therefore
[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]:A\to S
), and with the right-hand-sides of the equations pictured below:

\textstyle{A_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{k}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}F[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}]}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}(\bullet_{F})}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}S={\mathsf{T}}_{S}^{2}1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}1=S}

Remark 13
.

Given that
{\mathsf{e}}_{\alpha}
is defined as a
nested
fixpoint whereas the
{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\alpha}
of Definition
2.6
is defined as the solution of a similar equational system, but with all variables being
\nu
-variables, it follows immediately that
{\mathsf{e}}_{\alpha}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\alpha}
.

The main result of
[
CSH17
]
is an automata-theoretic characterisation of the semantics of the logics
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
. Below,
{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi)
is the
closure
of a formula
\varphi
, defined as usual for fixpoint logics, and the
product
between a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
and a quantitative parity automaton
(A,\alpha,\Omega)
is another quantitative parity automaton obtained by endowing the product of Definition
2.6
with parities inherited from
(A,\alpha,\Omega)
.

{thmC}

[
[
CSH17
]
]

If
({\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\beta,\Omega)
with
{\mathsf{ran}}(\Omega)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
is the parity automaton for a
clean
6
6

6

A formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
is said to be
clean
if no variable appears both free and bound, or is bound more than once, in
\varphi
.
and
strictly guarded
7
7

7

A formula is said to be
strictly guarded
if every fixpoint variable is immediately preceded by a modal operator.
formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
,
(C,\gamma)
is a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra and
{\mathsf{e}}_{\alpha}=[({\mathsf{e}}_{n})_{n\in{\mathsf{ran}}(\Omega)}]:A\to S
is the extent of the product parity automaton
(A,\alpha,\Omega)
of
(C,\gamma)
and
({\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\beta,\Omega)
, then
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}(c)={\mathsf{e}}_{\alpha}(c,\varphi)
for
c\in C
.

While the logics in
[
CSH17
]
lack the propositional constant
\top
, the results in loc. cit. can easily be generalised by including
\top
in
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
. This is because the formula
\top
corresponds to a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra automaton with a single state (with even parity) and a
\lambda
-transition with weight
1\in S
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
. The fact that
\Lambda
may be infinite is not an issue, since taking a product with a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra will yield a coalgebra with finite branching. It is easy to see that each formula in
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
is equivalent to a strictly guarded one: since modal operators are the only non-nullary operators in
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, the only way a clean formula can fail to be strictly guarded is when it contains a sub-formula of the form
\eta x.y
. Such a sub-formula can be replaced by
y
(if
x\neq y
) or
\bot
(if
x=y
and
\eta=\mu
) or
\top
(if
x=y
and
\eta=\nu
).

4.
Semiring-Valued Measures

This section gives a generalisation of the notion of (real-valued) measure on a
\sigma
-algebra (see e.g.
[
ADD99
]
) to measures valued into a partial semiring, and shows how standard measure extension results generalise to this setting.

Throughout this section, we fix a partial commutative semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
satisfying Assumption
2.1
. For such a semiring, we define a countable (partial) addition operation on
S
as an extension of the binary semiring addition:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}:=\sup\limits_{n\in\omega}(s_{0}+\ldots+s_{n})

(6)

If
S
is partial, the above countable sum is defined iff all sums
s_{0}+\ldots+s_{n}
with
n\in\omega
are defined. The definition exploits the fact that
s\sqsubseteq s+t
for any
s,t\in S
for which
s+t
is defined, together with the existence of joins of increasing countable chains (see Assumption
2.1
).

Remark 14
.

It follows immediately from the definition of countable sums that
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{f(i)}
for any bijection
f:\omega\to\omega
.

Remark 15
.

It also follows from the definition of countable sums, together with the distributivity of
\bullet
over finite sums and the preservation of joins of increasing countable chains by
\bullet
in each argument (Assumption
2.1
), that
\bullet
distributes over countable sums; that is, whenever
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}
is defined, then so is
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}(s\bullet s_{i})
and moreover,
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}(s\bullet s_{i})=s\bullet\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}
. This is because when
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}
is defined, all the partial sums
s_{0}+\ldots+s_{n}
are also defined, and therefore so is
s\bullet s_{0}+\ldots+s\bullet s_{n}=s\bullet(s_{0}+\ldots+s_{n})
, for all
n\in\omega
; this, in turn, gives that
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}(s\bullet s_{i})
is defined and equal to
\sup\limits_{n\in\omega}(s\bullet(s_{0}+\ldots+s_{n}))=s\bullet\sup\limits_{n\in\omega}(s_{0}+\ldots+s_{n})=s\bullet\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}
.

A further assumption on the semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
is needed to develop the theory of
S
-valued measures. In particular, this is needed to prove countable sub-additivity of our semiring-valued measures. The results in Sections
5
and
6
also rely on this assumption.

{asm}

We assume that
\bullet
preserves both suprema and infima in each argument, and that the following holds for all
A_{i}\subseteq S
with
i\in\omega
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}=\inf\big\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega,\,\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\text{ is defined\,}\big\}

(7)

whenever
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}
is defined.

Remark 16
.

The following always holds when
(S,\sqsubseteq)
is a complete lattice:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}\sqsubseteq\inf\big\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega,\,\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\text{ is defined\,}\big\}

provided that
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}
is defined. Assumption
4
strengthens this to an equality.

Remark 17
.

We verify that our example semirings (see Example
2.1
) satisfy Assumption
4
.

(1)

We first consider the case
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
. Clearly, the equality (
7
) holds when
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}=1
– one direction follows from Remark
16
and the other is immediate. When
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}<1
, the fact that
1
is a limit point can be used to construct
c_{i}\in[0,1]
with
i\in\omega
such that
\inf A_{i}<c_{i}
for
i\in\omega
and
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}c_{i}
is defined. For this, let
b_{0}>0
be such that
(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i})<(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}+b_{0})<1
. Now assuming
b_{0},\ldots,b_{i}
have been defined in such a way that
(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}~+\sum\limits_{j\in\{0,\ldots,i\}}b_{j})<1
, let
b_{i+1}>0
be such that
(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}+\sum\limits_{j\in\{0,\ldots,i\}}b_{j})<(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}+\sum\limits_{j\in\{0,\ldots,i+1\}}b_{j})<1
; finally, let
c_{i}=\inf A_{i}+b_{i}
for
i\in\omega
. We now have that
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}c_{i}=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}+\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}b_{i}
is defined since
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}+\sum\limits_{j\in\{0,\ldots,i\}}b_{j}
is defined for all
i\in\omega
. Then, since
\inf A_{i}<c_{i}
for
i\in\omega
and since
\leq
is a
total
order on
[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\inf A_{i}=\inf\{a_{i}\in A_{i}\mid c_{i}\not\leq a_{i}\}=\inf\{a_{i}\in A_{i}\mid a_{i}<c_{i}\}\text{ for }i\in\omega\,.

The required inequality now follows from:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf A_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf\{a_{i}\in A_{i}\mid a_{i}<c_{i}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i},\,a_{i}<c_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega\,\}

\displaystyle\sqsupseteq\inf\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega,\,\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\text{ is defined\,}\}

where the second equality above is a consequence of all the sums
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}
with
a_{i}<c_{i}
being defined in
[0,1]
(since
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}c_{i}
is defined) and of the same equality holding over the positive reals, whereas the inequality follows from
\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i},\,a_{i}<c_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega\,\}\subseteq\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega,\,\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\text{ is defined\,}\}
.

(2)

Second, we consider the case
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
; in this case,
\sqsubseteq
is the
\geq
relation on
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
and
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}
is given by
\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}
. Then, the required inequality instantiates to:

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}\sup A_{i}\leq\sup\big\{\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}a_{i}\mid a_{i}\in A_{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega\big\}

That this is true now follows by a relatively straighforward case analysis on whether the lhs equals
\infty
. A similar argument applies to bounded variants of the tropical semiring.

(3)

Finally, in the case when
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the proof is straightforward.

{defi}

A collection of sets
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}X
is a
\sigma
-algebra
provided that:

(1)

\emptyset\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

(2)

if
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
then
X\setminus A\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

(3)

if
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}
for
i\in\omega
, then
\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

Thus, a
\sigma
-algebra is also closed under (finite and) countable intersections (as
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}=X\setminus\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}(X\setminus A_{i})
). Also, if
A,B\in{\mathcal{A}}
, then
B\setminus A=B\cap(X\setminus A)\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

The next definition generalises real-valued measures on
\sigma
-algebras to measures valued into a partial semiring
(S,+,0,\bullet,1)
.

{defi}
[
S
-valued measure]

An
S
-valued measure
on a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is a function
\mu:{\mathcal{A}}\to S
such that:

•

\mu(\emptyset)=0
,

•

if
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}
for
i\in\omega
are pairwise disjoint, then
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
is defined and moreover,

\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
.

It follows immediately that any
S
-valued measure on
{\mathcal{A}}
is monotone, that is,
\mu(A)\sqsubseteq\mu(B)
whenever
A,B\in{\mathcal{A}}
are such that
A\subseteq B
: in this case,
\mu(B)=\mu(A)+\mu(B\setminus A)\sqsupseteq\mu(A)
. Moreover, as for standard measures, the following holds.

Proposition 18
.

If
\mu:{\mathcal{A}}\to S
is an
S
-valued measure on a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}
with
i\in\omega
are such that
A_{i}\subseteq A_{i+1}
for
i\in\omega
, then
\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\sup_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
.

Proof 4.1
.

We have
\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\mu(A_{0}\cup(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})\cup(A_{2}\setminus A_{1})\cup\ldots)=\mu(A_{0})+\mu(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})+\mu(A_{2}\setminus A_{1})+\ldots=\sup_{i\in\omega}(\mu(A_{0})+\mu(A_{1}\setminus A_{0})+\ldots+\mu(A_{i}\setminus A_{i-1}))=\sup_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
.

Next, we recall the notions of
semi-ring
8
8

8

The terminology clash with the notion of semiring should not cause any problems, given the different contexts in which
semirings
(like
S
) and
semi-rings
(of sets) are used.
,
ring
and
field of sets
, and show how
S
-valued measures on
\sigma
-algebras arise from certain
S
-valued functions on semi-rings.

{defi}

A collection of sets
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}X
is a
semi-ring
provided that:

(1)

\emptyset\in{\mathcal{S}}
,

(2)

if
A,B\in{\mathcal{S}}
, then
A\cap B\in{\mathcal{S}}
,

(3)

if
A,B\in{\mathcal{S}}
, there exist
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\in{\mathcal{S}}
pairwise disjoint such that
A\setminus B=A_{1}\cup\ldots\cup A_{n}
.

A non-empty collection of sets
{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}X
is a
ring
provided that:

(1)

if
A,B\in{\mathcal{R}}
, then
A\cup B\in{\mathcal{R}}
,

(2)

if
A,B\in{\mathcal{R}}
, then
A\setminus B\in{\mathcal{R}}
.

A ring
{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}X
which contains
X
is called a
field
.

Thus, every ring is a semi-ring. Moreover, given a semi-ring
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}X
, the smallest ring containing
{\mathcal{S}}
(defined as the intersection of
all
rings containing
{\mathcal{S}}
) is obtained by closing
{\mathcal{S}}
under finite unions of pairwise disjoint sets. That this yields a ring (i.e. it is also closed under relative complement) follows easily from
(\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})\setminus(\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{m}B_{j})=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigcap\limits_{j=1}^{m}(A_{i}\setminus B_{j})
together with the distributivity of finite intersections over finite unions.

The next definition generalises the notion of measure on a ring (see e.g.
[
ADD99
]
) to
S
-valued
measures.

{defi}

An
S
-valued measure on a ring
{\mathcal{R}}
is a function
\mu:{\mathcal{R}}\to S
such that:

•

\mu(\emptyset)=0
,

•

if
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
for
i\in\omega
are pairwise disjoint and
\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
, then
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
is defined and moreover,

\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
.

Remark 19
.

We note that any
S
-valued measure on a ring is
finitely
additive, that is, if
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\in{\mathcal{R}}
are pairwise disjoint, then
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu(A_{i})
is defined and moreover,
\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu(A_{i})
– this follows by extending the finite family
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
to a countable family
(A_{i})_{i\in\omega}
, where
A_{i}=\emptyset
for
i>n
.

The following is an immediate consequence of Definition
4
.

Proposition 20
.

Let
\mu:{\mathcal{R}}\to S
be an
S
-valued measure on a ring
{\mathcal{R}}
, and let
A_{1}\subseteq A_{2}\subseteq\ldots
be such that
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
for
i\in\omega
and also
\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}\in{\mathcal{R}}
. Then,
\sup_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})=\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})
.

Proof 4.2
.

Immediate from the definition of an
S
-valued measure on a ring, by considering the pairwise disjoint family
(A_{i}\setminus(A_{0}\cup\ldots\cup A_{i-1}))_{i\in\omega}
.

The following generalisation of a standard measure theory result to
S
-valued measures can also be proved.

Proposition 21
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}X
be a semi-ring and let
\mu:{\mathcal{S}}\to S
be such that:

(1)

\mu(\emptyset)=0
,

(2)

whenever
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{S}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
are pairwise disjoint, the sum
\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu(A_{i})
is defined,

(3)

whenever
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{S}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
are pairwise disjoint and moreover,
\bigcup\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}A_{i}\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}A_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu(A_{i})
.

Then
\mu
has a unique extension to an
S
-valued measure on the smallest ring
{\mathcal{R}}
containing
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof 4.3
.

Each
A\in{\mathcal{R}}
can be written as
\bigcup\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}A_{i}
with
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{S}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
pairwise disjoint. We let
\mu(A)=\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu(A_{i})
. To prove the independence of this definition on the choice of
(A_{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}
, let
B_{j}\in{\mathcal{S}}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
be pairwise disjoint and such that
A=\bigcup\limits_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}B_{j}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu(A_{i})=\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\sum\limits_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}\mu(A_{i}\cap B_{j})=\sum\limits_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu(A_{i}\cap B_{j})=\sum\limits_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}\mu(B_{j})

with all the sums above being defined. That
\mu
as defined above is an
S
-valued measure on
{\mathcal{R}}
is immediate.

{defi}

A
\sigma
-ring
is a ring which is closed under countable unions.

Thus, a
\sigma
-ring which contains
X
is a
\sigma
-algebra.

Given a ring
{\mathcal{R}}
, we write
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{R}})
for the
\sigma
-ring generated by
{\mathcal{R}}
. We also write
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
for the smallest subset of
{\mathcal{P}}X
which contains
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{R}})
and is closed under taking subsets. It is not difficult to show that
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
is itself a
\sigma
-ring (see e.g.
[
Bar03
, Section 5.1]
). Moreover, if
{\mathcal{R}}
is a field, then
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{R}})
is a
\sigma
-algebra and
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})={\mathcal{P}}X
.

We show in what follows that every
S
-valued measure on a field extends to an
S
-valued measure on the induced
\sigma
-algebra. A generalisation of the standard notion of
outer measure
(see e.g.
[
ADD99
, Definition 1.3.4]
or
[
RF10
, Section 17.3]
) will be used to derive this result.

{defi}

[
S
-valued outer measure]

Let
{\mathcal{R}}
be a ring. A function
\mu^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})\to S
is an
S
-valued outer measure
provided that:

(1)

\mu^{*}(\emptyset)=0
,

(2)

if
A,B\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
and
A\subseteq B
then
\mu^{*}(A)\sqsubseteq\mu^{*}(B)
,

(3)

if
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
for
i\in\omega
are pairwise disjoint and
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
is defined, then
\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
.

Remark 22
.

In order to deal with the case when
S
is a
partial
semiring (in which case, given a countable collection
(A_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}}))_{i\in\omega}
,
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
may not be defined, even if the
A_{i}
s are pairwise disjoint), the above definition differs slightly from the standard definition of an outer measure. However, when
S
is a
total
semiring, condition (
3
) of Definition
4
generalises the standard one (see.e.g.
[
ADD99
, Def. 1.3.4]
) – note that requiring it for pairwise disjoint, countable collections of subsets means that it also holds for arbitrary countable collections of subsets. Also, when
S
is the (partial) probabilistic semiring, condition (
3
) of Definition
4
is equivalent to the standard one used in the definition of outer probability measures, since whenever for a countable collection
(A_{i})_{i\in\omega}
the sum
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
is not defined (in
[0,1]
), the required inequality holds automatically in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

The next definition also generalises a standard one (see e.g.
[
ADD99
, Section1.3]
or
[
RF10
, Section 17.3]
).

{defi}

Given an
S
-valued outer measure
\mu^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})\to S
, we call a set
E\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})

\mu^{*}
-measurable
if for every
A\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
, the sum
\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))
is defined and moreover,

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(A)=\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))\,.

(8)

Remark 23
.

At first sight, the above definition also differs from the standard definition of measurability induced by an outer measure when the semiring
S
is partial. However, note that in the case of the probabilistic semiring, the standard definition, which considers the rhs of (
8
) in Definition
4
as an element of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, forces the sum in the rhs to belong to
[0,1]
; thus, in this case the two definitions are equivalent.

The next result now generalises a standard one from measure theory.

Proposition 24
.

If
\mu^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})\to S
is an
S
-valued outer measure, then:

(1)

The collection
{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mu^{*}
-measurable subsets forms a
\sigma
-algebra.

(2)

The restriction of
\mu^{*}
to
{\mathcal{M}}
is an
S
-valued measure.

Proof 4.4
.

In spite of our slightly different definition of outer measure, the proof is exactly the same as the standard one (see e.g.
[
RF10
, Section 17.4]
): one first shows that
{\mathcal{M}}
is closed under finite unions of pairwise disjoint subsets and that
\mu^{*}
is finitely additive on
{\mathcal{M}}
, and then extends this to countable unions of pairwise disjoint sets and countable additivity. The requirement of Definition
4
that the sum in the rhs of (
8
) is defined means that all the sums appearing in the standard proof are defined.

We are now ready to show how an
S
-valued measure on a field extends to an
S
-valued measure on the induced
\sigma
-algebra. As in standard measure theory, this is done with the help of an outer measure.

{defi}

Let
{\mathcal{R}}
be a ring and let
\mu:{\mathcal{R}}\to S
be an
S
-valued measure on
{\mathcal{R}}
. The
outer measure
\mu^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})\to S
induced by
\mu
is given by

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(A)=\inf\{\,\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n})\mid(E_{n}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega}\text{ pairwise disjoint},~A\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n}\}

Note that the pairwise disjointness of
(E_{n})_{n\in\omega}
together with
{\mathcal{R}}
being a ring means that each sum
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mu(E_{i})
with
n\in\omega
is defined (see Remark
19
), and therefore so is
\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n})
. Also, note that the infima needed in Definition
4
exist as a result of
(S,\sqsubseteq)
being a complete lattice (see Assumption
2.1
).

Proposition 25
.

(1)

\mu^{*}
is an outer measure on
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
.

(2)

\mu^{*}(E)=\mu(E)
for
E\in{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof 4.5
.

For the first statement, the fact that
\mu^{*}(\emptyset)=0
is immediate, and so is the monotonicity of
\mu^{*}
. (For the latter, (
2
) of Remark
1
is needed.) The proof of countable sub-additivity of
\mu^{*}
rests on Assumption
4
. To show countable sub-additivity, fix pairwise disjoint sets
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
with
i\in\omega
, such that
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
is defined. To show
\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
, let
(E_{n}^{i}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega}
be pairwise disjoint and such that
A_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n}^{i}
, for each
i\in\omega
. Now view
(E_{n}^{i}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega,i\in\omega}
as a single countable family
(F_{k})_{k\in\omega}
, and consider the countable,
pairwise disjoint
family
(F^{\prime}_{k}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{k\in\omega}
given by
F^{\prime}_{k}=F_{k}\setminus(F_{0}\cup\ldots\cup F_{k-1})
for
k\in\omega
. (Recall that
{\mathcal{R}}
is a ring and therefore
F^{\prime}_{k}\in{\mathcal{R}}
for
k\in\omega
.) Then,
\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}F_{k}=\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}F^{\prime}_{k}
, and therefore, by definition of
\mu^{*}
,
\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{k\in\omega}\mu(F^{\prime}_{k})
. At the same time, monotonicity of
\mu
gives
\sum\limits_{k\in\omega}\mu(F^{\prime}_{k})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{k\in\omega}\mu(F_{k})=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})
whenever
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})
is defined. (For the latter equality, Remark
14
is used.) Hence,
\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})
whenever the latter sum is defined. As this holds for every choice of
(E_{n}^{i}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega}
with
(E_{n}^{i})_{n\in\omega}
pairwise disjoint and
A_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n}^{i}
with
i\in\omega
for which
\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})
is defined, we now have:

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\inf\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})\mid(E_{n}^{i}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega}\text{ pairwise disjoint},\,A_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n}^{i}\text{ for }i\in\omega,~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})\text{ is defined\,}\}

The above together with Assumption
4
, gives:

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\inf\{\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n}^{i})\mid(E_{n}^{i}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega}\text{ pairwise disjoint},\,A_{i}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n}^{i}\}

That is,
\mu^{*}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu^{*}(A_{i})
.

For the second statement, the fact that
\{E\}
is a cover for
E\in{\mathcal{R}}
immediately gives
\mu^{*}(E)\sqsubseteq\mu(E)
. To show
\mu(E)\sqsubseteq\mu^{*}(E)
, we must show
\mu(E)\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n})
for each pairwise disjoint family
(E_{n}\in{\mathcal{R}})_{n\in\omega}
such that
E\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n}
. Given such a family
(E_{n})_{n\in\omega}
, the family
(E\cap(E_{0}\cup\ldots\cup E_{n}))_{n\in\omega}
satisfies
\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}(E\cap(E_{0}\cup\ldots\cup E_{n}))=E\cap(\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}E_{n})=E\in{\mathcal{R}}
, and then Proposition
20
gives
\sup_{n\in\omega}\mu(E\cap(E_{0}\cup\ldots\cup E_{n}))=\mu(E)
. On the other hand,
\mu(E\cap(E_{0}\cup\ldots\cup E_{n}))\sqsubseteq\mu(E_{0}\cup\ldots\cup E_{n})=\sum\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu(E_{i})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n})
for all
n\in\omega
. Putting these together we have
\mu(E)=\sup_{n\in\omega}\mu(E\cap(E_{0}\cup\ldots\cup E_{n}))\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu(E_{n})
as required.

We now proceed as in the standard case to show that if
\mu^{*}
is as in Definition
4
, then the elements of
{\mathcal{R}}
are
\mu^{*}
-measurable. For this, we additionally assume that
{\mathcal{R}}
is a field.

Proposition 26
.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}
be a field, and let
\mu:{\mathcal{R}}\to S
and
\mu^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})\to S
be as in Definition
4
. Then each
E\in{\mathcal{R}}
is
\mu^{*}
-measurable.

Proof 4.6
.

We must show that for each
A\in{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})
, the sum
\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))
is defined and moreover,
\mu^{*}(A)=\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))
.

Since
\{E\}
is a finite (and therefore countable) cover for
A\cap E
, and
\{X\setminus E\}
is a finite cover for
A\cap(X\setminus E)
, it immediately follows that
\mu^{*}(A\cap E)\sqsubseteq\mu(E)
and
\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))\sqsubseteq\mu(X\setminus E)
. Definedness of
\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))
now follows by (
2
) of Remark
1
. Moreover, since
\mu^{*}
is an outer measure and
A\cap E)\cap(A\cap(X\setminus E))=\emptyset
, we immediately get
\mu^{*}(A)\sqsubseteq\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))
. For the reverse direction, note that any cover
(E_{i})_{i\in\omega}
for
A
consisting of pairwise disjoint elements of
{\mathcal{R}}
yields a cover
(E_{i}\cap E)_{i\in\omega}
for
A\cap E
and a cover
(E_{i}\cap(X\setminus E))_{i\in\omega}
for
A\cap(X\setminus E)
, each consisting of pairwise disjoint elements. Then,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(E_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}(\mu(E_{i}\cap E)+\mu(E_{i}\cap(X\setminus E)))

(Remark
19
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(E_{i}\cap E)+\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(E_{i}\cap(X\setminus E))

(Remark
14
)

\displaystyle\sqsupseteq\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))

The above together with the definition of
\mu^{*}(A)
now gives
\mu^{*}(A)\sqsupseteq\mu^{*}(A\cap E)+\mu^{*}(A\cap(X\setminus E))
. This concludes the proof.

We are now ready to formulate our generalised measure extension result.

Theorem 27
.

Let
\mu:{\mathcal{R}}\to S
be a measure on a field
{\mathcal{R}}
. Then,
\mu
extends to a measure on the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof 4.7
.

By Proposition
25
, the
S
-valued function
\mu^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{R}})\to S
of Definition
4
is an outer measure which extends
\mu
. Then, by Proposition
24
, the set
{\mathcal{M}}
of
\mu^{*}
-measurable sets is a
\sigma
-algebra containing
{\mathcal{R}}
, and the restriction of
\mu^{*}
to
{\mathcal{M}}
is an
S
-valued measure. Finally, by Proposition
26
,
{\mathcal{M}}
contains
{\mathcal{R}}
, and therefore also the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
{\mathcal{R}}
. This makes
\mu^{*}
an
S
-valued measure on the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
{\mathcal{R}}
.

In the standard case, the uniqueness of an extension of a measure
\mu
on a field
{\mathcal{R}}
to a measure on the generated
\sigma
-algebra requires
\mu
to be
\sigma
-finite
(cf. Carathéodory’s extension theorem, see e.g.
[
ADD99
, Theorem 1.3.10]
). Under the
\sigma
-finiteness assumption, the induced measure additionally satisfies
\mu(\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})
with
A_{0}\supseteq A_{1}\supseteq\ldots
a decreasing countable family of measurable sets. (Recall from Proposition
18
that
S
-valued measures on
\sigma
-algebras always satisfy

\mu(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\sup_{i\in\omega}\mu(A_{i})

whenever
A_{0}\subseteq A_{1}\subseteq\ldots
is an increasing countable family of measurable sets.) Since the
S
-valued measures used later to provide a path-based semantics for our logics do not satisfy the equality
\mu_{j}(\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu_{j}(A_{i})
for all
A_{0}\supseteq A_{1}\supseteq\ldots
with
A_{i}
measurable for
i\in\omega
(see Example
5
), we are not interested in a uniqueness result generalising the above-mentioned result.

5.
Path-Based Semantics for Quantitative Linear-Time Logics

This section provides an alternative, path-based semantics for the logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, akin to the path-based semantics of LTL and the linear-time
\mu
-calculus (interpreted over either non-deterministic or probabilistic transition systems).

We begin by defining a
\sigma
-algebra structure on the set of paths from a given state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
. We subsequently show how to define an
S
-valued measure (Definition
4
) on this
\sigma
-algebra.

Similarly to the semantics of probabilistic LTL (see e.g.
[
BK08
, Section 10.3]
), our
\sigma
-algebra is induced by so-called
cylinder sets
. We begin by associating to each path fragment
q
in
(C,\gamma)
a cylinder set.

{defi}

Let
q\in I_{C}
be a path fragment from
c
in
(C,\gamma)
. Its associated
cylinder set

{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)=\{p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\mid q\in{\mathsf{pref}}(p)\}

We note that only paths whose transitions have non-zero weights in
(C,\gamma)
are considered.

Proposition 28
.

For
c\in C
, the set

\displaystyle{\Sigma}_{c}:=\{\emptyset\}\cup\{\,{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)\mid q\text{ is a path fragment from $c$ in $(C,\gamma)$}\,\}

is a semi-ring (Definition
4
). Moreover,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\in\Sigma_{c}
.

Proof 5.1
.

(1) of Definition
4
clearly holds. For (2), note that
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{1})\cap{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{2})
is either
\emptyset
, if
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are incompatible (Definition
2.2
), or
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
, if
q
is the smallest path fragment from
c
such that
q_{1}\in{\mathsf{pref}}(q)
and
q_{2}\in{\mathsf{pref}}(q)
, otherwise. For (3) of Definition
4
, assuming
A={\mathsf{Cyl}}(p)
and
B={\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
with
A\setminus B\neq\emptyset
, unfolding the coalgebra structure
\gamma
a number of times equal to
\max(0pt(p),0pt(q))
allows us to identify a
finite
number of path fragments
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
from
c
in
(C,\gamma)
, such that
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{i})\cap{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{j})=\emptyset
for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
i\neq j
, and such that
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(p)\setminus{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)={\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{1})\cup\ldots\cup{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{n})
. Finally,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\in\Sigma_{c}
follows from
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}={\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{c})
, where
q_{c}
is the path fragment given by
(c,\iota_{1}(*))
.

{defi}

For a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
and
c\in C
, the set
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}
of
measurable sets of paths from
c
is
the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\Sigma_{c}
(see Section
4
).

Equivalently,
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra induced by the smallest ring
{\mathcal{R}}_{c}
containing
\Sigma_{c}
. The elements of
{\mathcal{R}}_{c}
are finite unions of pairwise disjoint elements of
\Sigma_{c}
. Moreover, by Proposition
28
,
{\mathcal{R}}_{c}
is a field. Then, by Theorem
27
, any
S
-valued measure
\mu
on
{\mathcal{R}}_{c}
(and therefore any
S
-valued measure on
\Sigma_{c}
) extends to an
S
-valued measure
\mu^{*}
on the induced
\sigma
-algebra, constructed as in Section
4
with the help of an outer-measure.

{defi}

[Induced measure]

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra. For
c\in C
, let
\mu_{\gamma}:\Sigma_{c}\to S
be given by:

(1)

\mu_{\gamma}(\emptyset)=0
,

(2)

For
q
a path fragment from
c
in
(C,\gamma)
,
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q))
is defined by induction on the structure of
q
:

(a)

If
\iota^{-1}_{C}(q)=(c,\iota_{1}(*))
, then
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q))={\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}(c)
(the
\nu
-extent of Definition
2.6
),

(b)

If
\iota^{-1}_{C}(q)=(c,\iota_{\lambda}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))
for some
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
\pi_{1}(\iota^{-1}_{C}(q_{i}))=c_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
, then

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q))=\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{1}))\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))

Also, let
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{R}}_{c}\to S
be given by
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}C_{i})=\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\mu_{\gamma}(C_{i})
for each finite family
(C_{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}
with
C_{i}\in\Sigma_{c}
and
C_{i}\cap C_{j}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. The
induced measure

\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\to S
is the extension of
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{R}}_{c}\to S
to an
S
-valued measure on
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
, as given by the proof of Theorem
27
.

The correctness of the above definition (i.e. the existence of
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{R}}_{c}\to S
) is guaranteed by the additivity of
\mu_{\gamma}:\Sigma_{c}\to S
:

Proposition 29
.

Let
\mu_{\gamma}:\Sigma_{c}\to S
be as in Definiton
5
. Then,
\mu_{\gamma}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
21
.

Proof 5.2
.

That the first two assumptions are satisfied is an easy exercise (with induction and use of (
3
) of Remark
1
being needed for the second assumption). For the third assumption, let
q
be a path fragment from
c
in
(C,\gamma)
, and assume
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)={\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{1})\cup\ldots\cup{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{n})
, with
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{i})\cap{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{j})=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. Since for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{i})\subseteq{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
, an easy induction on
0pt(q)
gives
q\in{\mathsf{pref}}(q_{i})
. Moreover, since the cylinder sets
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{i})
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
are pairwise disjoint, we can assume w.l.o.g. that the path fragments
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
are pairwise incompatible: if this is not the case for
q_{i}
and
q_{j}
, one can always replace each of
q_{i}
and
q_{j}
with an equivalent set of path fragments of depth uniformly
\max(0pt(q_{i}),0pt(q_{j}))
, and subsequently remove the path fragments whose associated cylinder sets have measure
0
. The fact that
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q))=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{1}))+\ldots+\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{n}))
then follows by induction on
\max\{0pt(q_{i})\mid i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\,\}-0pt(q)
. In the base case, the path fragments
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
all extend
q
with at most one step in each leaf of
q
. Then, since the cylinder sets associated to
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
cover
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
, it follows that for all path fragments that extend
q
and are not subsumed by one of
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
, the associated cylinder sets are empty (and thus have measure
0
). This, together with the definition of
\nu
-extents, gives
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q))=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{1}))+\ldots+\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{n}))
. The induction step follows by applying the induction hypothesis to the path fragments
q^{1},\ldots,q^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
, where the tree associated to
q
has root given by
(c,\lambda)
and immediate sub-trees corresponding to
q^{1},\ldots,q^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
.

As a result,
\mu_{\gamma}:\Sigma_{c}\to S
extends to a measure on the field
{\mathcal{R}}_{c}
generated by
\Sigma_{c}
. We can now consider the
S
-valued measure
\mu_{\gamma}
on the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\Sigma_{c}
, given by (the restriction of) the induced outer measure
\mu_{\gamma}^{*}
. (Recall that we did not prove a uniqueness result for an
S
-valued measure extending
\mu_{\gamma}:\Sigma_{c}\to S
. At this point it is not clear to us whether such a result can be proved, and what additional assumptions on the semiring
S
this might require.) To avoid confusion with least fixpoint formulas, we will always use the subscript
\gamma
when referring to the resulting
S
-valued measure.

{exa}

Using Example
2.6
and Definition
5
, we now obtain the following instances of the resulting measure
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\to S
:

(1)

For
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
and
A\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
, we have
\mu_{\gamma}(A)=1
if and only if
A\neq\emptyset
.

(2)

For
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
and
A\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
,
\mu_{\gamma}(A)
gives the probability of exhibiting a path in
A
from state
c
. Note that in this case, under the assumption that the sum of the probabilities of outgoing transitions from each state equals
1
, the induced measure coincides with the measure used when interpreting LTL over
probabilistic transition systems
9
9

9

These are essentially discrete-time Markov chains, but without the initial distribution.
(see e.g.
[
BK08
, Section 10.3]
).

(3)

For
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
and
A\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
,
\mu_{\gamma}(A)
gives the minimal weight of a path in
A
from state
c
.

The next example shows that the resulting measure
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\to S
does
not
satisfy

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})=\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})

whenever
A_{n}
with
n\in\omega
are measurable sets such that
A_{0}\supseteq A_{1}\supseteq\ldots
.

{exa}

Let
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, and consider the
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ(\{a,b\}\times{\mathsf{Id}})
-coalgebra depicted below, (where all transitions shown have weight
1
):

\textstyle{c_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{d_{0}^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{c_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{d_{1}^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{d_{1}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{c_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{d_{2}^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{d_{2}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{d_{2}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\vdots}

Now consider the following sets of paths from
c_{0}
:

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.40685pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.40685pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{c_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.23802pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
