\displaystyle\ldots

with
A_{0}\supseteq A_{1}\supseteq\ldots
. That is, for
i\in\omega
,
A_{i}
consists of those paths from
c_{0}
containing at least
i+1

a
-labelled transitions. Then,
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}=\emptyset
and so
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i})=0
, however,
\mu_{\gamma}(A_{i})=1
for all
i\in\omega
, and therefore
\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{i})=1
.

The above equality holds, however, when each
A_{n}
is a
finite
union of cylinder sets.

Proposition 30
.

Let
A_{0}\supseteq A_{1}\supseteq\ldots
, with each
A_{n}
(
n\in\omega
) being given by a
finite
union of cylinder sets; that is,
A_{n}=\bigcup{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
with
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
consisting of finitely-many cylinder sets, for
n\in\omega
. Then

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})=\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})

Proof 5.3
.

Clearly,
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})\sqsubseteq\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})
:
\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\subseteq A_{n}
gives
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})\sqsubseteq\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})
for all
n\in\omega
, and therefore
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})\sqsubseteq\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})
.

To show that
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})\sqsupseteq\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})
also holds, recall from Definition
4
that
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})=\inf\{\,\sum\limits_{k\in\omega}\mu(E_{k})\mid(E_{k}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{c})_{k\in\omega}\text{ pairwise disjoint},~\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}E_{k}\}
, which, by the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}_{c}
, coincides with
\inf\{\,\sum\limits_{k\in\omega}\mu(B_{k})\mid(B_{k}\in\Sigma_{c})_{n\in\omega}\text{ pairwise disjoint},~\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}\}
. We now fix a countable family
(B_{k}\in\Sigma_{c})_{k\in\omega}
of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets with
\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
. We can assume w.l.o.g. that all cylinder sets
B_{k}
with
k\in\omega
have uniform depth, that is, when viewed as trees, all the paths from the root to the leaves of the tree have the same length. (Each cylinder set which does not have uniform depth can be replaced by a
finite
number of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets of uniform depth, with the measure of the union of these cylinder sets being equal to the measure of the original cylinder set.) We can similarly assume that each
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
consists of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets of uniform depth, and moreover, each cylinder set in
{\mathcal{A}}_{n+1}
is a subset of some cylinder set in
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
. (Overlaps between different cylinder sets belonging to some
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
can be removed by replacing the cylinder sets in question with finite disjoint unions of cylinder sets of larger depth. Also, whenever a cylinder set in
{\mathcal{A}}_{n+1}
is not included in a
single
cylinder set in
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
, the former can be replaced by a finite number of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets of larger depth, in such a way that the property is established.) We now show that, for each cylinder set
C\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, there exists
n_{C}\in\omega
such that all cylinder sets in
{\mathcal{A}}_{n_{C}}
which are included in
C
are also included in
\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
. For, if this was not the case, there would exist a decreasing sequence of cylinder sets
C=C_{0}\supseteq C_{1}\supseteq\ldots
with
C_{n}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
and
C_{n}\not\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
for
n\in\omega
– this follows by applying König’s lemma to the graph whose nodes are cylinder sets belonging to some
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
and included in
C
, but not included in
\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
, and whose edges are determined by the subset relation between cylinder sets at successive levels. This, in turn, would yield an element
z\in Z
such that
z\in\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}
(as
z\in C_{n}\subseteq A_{n}
for all
n\in\omega
) but
z\not\in\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
(as none of the cylinder sets
C_{i}
is contained in
\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
). Now by finiteness of
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, there exists
m\in\omega
such that
A_{m}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega}B_{k}
– simply take
m=\max\{n_{C}\mid C\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\}
. We now have:

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})\sqsubseteq\mu_{\gamma}(A_{m})\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{k\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(B_{k})

As the above holds for
any
choice of cylinder set cover
(B_{k})_{k\in\omega}
for
\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}
, we now obtain:

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})\sqsubseteq\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})

This concludes the proof.

Before defining the path-based semantics for the logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, we prove one more property of the
\sigma
-algebras
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
and of the associated measures
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\to S
with
c\in C
. This will be needed to show that the path-based semantics of modal formulas (of the form
\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
) agrees with their step-wise semantics.

Proposition 31
.

Let
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
c,c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}\in C
be such that
\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\neq 0
.
If
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c_{i}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
, then
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
. Moreover, the following holds:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])=\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\,.

Proof 5.4
.

The first statement is immediate in the case when one of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
is the empty set. Assume therefore that this is not the case (and thus also none of
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}
is the empty set). The statement now follows from
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]=\bigcap\limits_{i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}}(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{i}/c_{i},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
, together with each of the sets of paths in the above intersection belonging to
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
. To see why the latter is true, note that we can use the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}
to derive a
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
by taking

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}=\{A_{i}\subseteq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}\mid(c,\lambda)[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{i}/c_{i},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\}\,.

That this yields a
\sigma
-algebra follows from
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
being a
\sigma
-algebra: (i)
\emptyset\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
follows from
\emptyset\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
, (ii) closure of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
under complements follows from the fact that, for
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
, both
(c,\lambda)[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}/c_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
and
(c,\lambda)[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{i}/c_{i},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
belong to
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
, and therefore so does their set difference
(c,\lambda)[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}/c_{1},\ldots,({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}\setminus A_{i})/c_{i},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
, and (iii) closure of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
under countable unions follows from the closure of
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
under countable unions of sets of the form used in the definition of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
. Now
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
contains all cylinder sets in
\Sigma_{c_{i}}
(as
{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
contains all cylinder sets in
\Sigma_{c}
), and therefore
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}\supseteq{\mathcal{M}}_{c_{i}}
(as the latter is the
smallest
such
\sigma
-algebra). Thus,
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c_{i}}
implies
A_{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{c_{i}}
implies
(c,\lambda)[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{i}/c_{i},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
as required.

The proof of the second statement is less straightforward. We reduce proving the equality to proving two inequalities.

(1)

For the ”
\sqsubseteq
” part, we show that the following holds for any choice of countable, disjoint cylinder set covers
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
for
A_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])\sqsubseteq\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}}_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\,.

For this, note that the covers
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
yield a countable, disjoint cylinder set cover
{\mathcal{C}}
for
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
, with each
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
given by
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
where
q
is a path fragment with root
(c,\lambda)
and sub-trees
q_{1},\ldots,q_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}
, with
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q_{i})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
. It then follows from the definition of
\mu_{\gamma}
on cylinder sets (Definition
5
) together with the distributivity of
\bullet
over countable sums (Remark
15
) that
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}})=\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}}_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
. This gives the above inequality. Moreover, since this inequality holds for an arbitrary choice of
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
s, and since
\bullet
preserves infima in each argument (see Assumption
4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])\sqsubseteq\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})

as required.

(2)

For the ”
\sqsupseteq
” part, we let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a countable, disjoint cylinder set cover for the set
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
. The obvious way to proceed would be to construct cylinder set covers
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
for
A_{i}
, such that
{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[{\mathcal{C}}_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
. However, this turns out to be difficult, if at all possible. Instead, we will construct a decreasing sequence of covers
\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{0}\supseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{1}\supseteq\ldots
for each
A_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
, additionally satisfying
{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})/c_{1},\ldots,
(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n})/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
, and use this inclusion to show the required inequality.

We assume w.l.o.g. that
{\mathcal{C}}
only contains cylinder sets of uniform depth, listed in increasing depth order, and that
{\mathcal{C}}
is
minimal
, in that no proper subset of
{\mathcal{C}}
covers
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
. (Any cylinder set in
{\mathcal{C}}
with non-uniform depth can be replaced by a
finite
number of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets of uniform depth, with the measure of the union of these cylinder sets being equal to the measure of the original cylinder set. Also, cylinder sets (of uniform depth) that are not needed to cover
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
can be removed from
{\mathcal{C}}
one by one to obtain a minimal cover; each such removal can only decrease the value of
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}})
.) Now assuming
{\mathcal{C}}
consists of cylinder sets
B_{j}={\mathsf{Cyl}}((c,\lambda)(q_{1}^{j},\ldots,q_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}^{j}))
with
j\in\omega
, we have, for
j,k\in\omega
with
j<k
and for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
, that either
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\cap{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{k}_{i})=\emptyset
or
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\supseteq{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{k}_{i})
. A key observation then is that, if
B_{j},B_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}
are such that
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\supseteq{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{k}_{i})
, then since
B_{j}\cap B_{k}=\emptyset
, and since each of
B_{j},B_{k}
is needed to cover the set
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
, the set
A_{i}
can only contain a path in
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\setminus{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{k}_{i})
if this is witnessed by some other
B_{l}
such that
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\supseteq{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{l}_{i})
; in other words, the cylinder set
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})
is not really needed to construct a cover of
A_{i}
, and considering instead
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{k}_{i})
and any additional cylinder sets
{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{l}_{i})
as above is sufficient. Moreover, this applies to
any
pair of cylinder sets
B_{j},B_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}
as above. Then, a decreasing sequence of finite covers of cylinder sets for each
A_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
, is obtained through the following steps:

(a)

The countable set of cylinder sets
\{{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\mid j\in\omega\}
is partitioned into
finite
collections
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}=\{{\mathsf{Cyl}}(q^{j}_{i})\mid j\in\omega,\,0pt(q^{j}_{i})=n\}
, with
n\in\omega
.

(b)

The collections
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}
with
n\in\omega
are extended as follows:

•

for
n=0,1,\ldots
, if
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n+1}
does not contain
any
subset of some cylinder set in
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}
, then that cylinder set is added to
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n+1}
.

•

for
n=0,1,\ldots
, if
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}
does not contain any
superset
of some cylinder set in
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n+1}
, then that cylinder set is added to
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}
.

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
, the resulting collections
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}^{n}
with
n\in\omega
are still finite, and such that
\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{0}\supseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{1}\supseteq\ldots
. Moreover, this decreasing sequence is such that
\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}\supseteq A_{i}
(because of the key observation above), and satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
30
(which in turn gives
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n})=\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n})
). Finally, our construction of the sets
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}
with
n\in\omega
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
gives

\displaystyle(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})/c_{1},\ldots,(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n})/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}\,.

We now have:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}})

\displaystyle\sqsupseteq\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})/c_{1},\ldots,(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n})/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])

(monotonicity of
\mu_{\gamma}
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n}/c_{1},\ldots,\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])

\displaystyle=\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n}/c_{1},\ldots,\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])

(*)

\displaystyle=\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\left(\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n})\right)

(**)

\displaystyle=\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}(\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n}))

\displaystyle\sqsupseteq\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})\bullet\ldots\bullet\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n})

\displaystyle=\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n})

\displaystyle\sqsupseteq\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})

(monotonicity of
\mu_{\gamma}
)

In the above, (*) follows from the application of Proposition
30
, after noting that the sets
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n}/c_{1},\ldots,\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
with
n\in\omega
satisfy the hypothesis of this result (by finiteness of each
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{i}
). Also, (**) follows from the definition of
\mu_{\gamma}
on finite disjoint unions of cylinder sets and the distributivity of
\bullet
over finite sums, after noting that each of
{\mathcal{C}}^{i}_{n}
as well as
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{1}_{n}/c_{1},\ldots,\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}_{n}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
can be written as finite disjoint unions of cylinder sets. Now as the above sequence of (in)equalities holds for any disjoint cylinder set cover
{\mathcal{C}}
for
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[A_{1}/c_{1},\ldots,A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}])\sqsupseteq\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{1})\bullet\ldots\bullet\mu_{\gamma}(A_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})

as required. This concludes the proof of the ”
\sqsupseteq
” part and the proof of the proposition.

We now observe that
({\mathsf{Paths}}_{C},\pi_{2}\circ\zeta_{C})
is an
F
-coalgebra, and therefore there is a standard notion of satisfaction of formulas in
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
by states of this coalgebra, i.e. by paths in
C
(see Section
2.7
). In particular, under this standard semantics, any state of an
F
-coalgebra satisfies the formula
\top
. We are finally ready to define a path-based semantics for the logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
.

{defi}

[Path-based semantics for
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
]

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra. The
path-based semantics

\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}\in S^{C}
of a formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
in
(C,\gamma)
is given by
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}(c)=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi))
for
c\in C
, where
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)=\{p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\mid p\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\pi_{2}\circ\zeta_{C}}\}
.

Remark 32
.

As expected, when
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
, the measure of Definition
5
associates a non-zero value to a measurable set of paths precisely when the set in question is non-empty. When
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
, our semantics is similar to the probabilistic semantics of LTL, which also involves defining a
\sigma
-algebra structure on the set of maximal paths from a given state
[
BK08
, Chapter 10]
. However, differently from probabilistic systems, the definition of the
S
-valued measure induced by a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra has a coinductive flavour, given our use of
\nu
-extents to measure cylinder sets. When the sum of probabilities of outgoing transitions from each state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra is
1
, our definition of the induced measure is similar to the standard one, which only takes into account the probabilities of transitions that match a given path fragment
q
, as in this case the
\nu
-extent of each state is equal to
1
. However, when the above condition is not satisfied, or when
S
is a
different
(e.g. the tropical) semiring, our definition takes into account not just the weights of transitions that match a path fragment
q
, but also the future linear-time behaviour (
\nu
-extent) of the states annotating the leaves of
q
. This is natural, given our emphasis on
maximal
(rather than finite) traces.

Definition
5
thus generalises existing path-based semantics for non-deterministic and probabilistic systems. The remainder of this section shows the correctness of this definition, by proving that the set
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
is indeed measurable for each
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
and
c\in C
. To this end, we associate sets of paths not just to formulas in
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, but also to formulas in
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
with
\mathcal{V}
a set of variables and
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
a valuation. Then we show that, under certain assumptions on
V
, those sets of paths are measurable. This, in turn, yields measurability of
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
. Our proof makes use of the concept of
fixpoint nesting depth
.

{defi}

The
fixpoint nesting depth
of a formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
, denoted
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)
, is defined by induction on the structure of
\varphi
:

•

If
\varphi=\bot
or
\varphi=\top
or
\varphi=x
with
x\in\mathcal{V}
, then
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)=0
.

•

If
\varphi=\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
, then
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)=\max\{{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{i})\mid i\in I\}
. The definition in the case of modalities incorporating guarded disjunctions is similar.

•

If
\varphi=\eta x.\varphi_{x}
, then
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)={\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x})+1
.

We now fix a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
. For
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
,
c\in C
and
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
, we let
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi):=\{p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\mid p\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\pi_{2}\circ\zeta_{C}}^{V}\}
.
For conciseness, in what follows we write
\zeta^{\prime}_{C}
for
\pi_{2}\circ\zeta_{C}:{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}\to F{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}
.

Proposition 33
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra, and let
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
. If
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
is such that the set
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(x)=\{p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}\mid V(x)(p)=1\}
is measurable for all
c\in C
and
x\in\mathcal{V}
, then the set
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)
is measurable for all
c\in C
.

Proof 5.5
.

Induction on
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)
.

(1)

{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)=0
. We prove the statement by structural induction on
\varphi
:

(a)

\varphi=\bot
. In this case
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)=\emptyset\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
.

(b)

\varphi=\top
. In this case
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)={\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{c}
.

(c)

\varphi=x\in\mathcal{V}
. In this case the statement follows from the assumption on
V
.

(d)

\varphi=\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
with
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}^{V}(\varphi_{i})
measurable for each
c_{i}\in C
and each
i\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
. Then,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))
is given by the finite union

\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\neq 0}(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}^{V}(\varphi_{1})/c_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}^{V}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}(\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]

That each of
(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{1}}^{V}(\varphi_{1})/c_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{Paths}}^{V}_{c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}(\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})/c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}]
is measurable follows from the induction hypothesis by Proposition
31
.

(2)

{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)>0
. As for case (
1
), we prove the statement by structural induction on
\varphi
, with four similar sub-cases treated in exactly the same way, but with two additional cases, considered below:

(e)

\varphi=\mu x.\varphi_{x}
. In this case, it follows from the continuity of the operator used to define
\llbracket\mu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V}
(see Remark
11
) that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)=\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})

(12)

where
\varphi_{x}^{(0)}=\bot
and
\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}=\varphi_{x}[\varphi_{x}^{(i)}/x]
for
i\in\omega
10
10

10

In the above, we assume that
x\not\in\mathcal{V}
. This can easily be guaranteed by performing some variable renamings whenever the formula in question is not clean.
. Note that, at this point, we cannot immediately use the induction hypothesis, since in general
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x}^{(i)})\not<{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)
– take, for instance,
\varphi:=\mu x.\nu y.(\langle a\rangle(x)\sqcup\langle b\rangle y)
, for which
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x}^{(2)})=2
. We proceed in the following way: we prove by induction on
i\in\omega
that
\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
is measurable and
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
is measurable.

(i)

i=0
. In this case,
\varphi_{x}^{(i)}=\bot
and hence
\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}=\emptyset
is measurable. Also,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(x)={\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\lambda z.0\}}(x)=\emptyset
is measurable. Then, since
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x})={\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)-1
, and since
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(y)
is measurable (as it coincides with
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(y)
) for
y\in\mathcal{V}
, it follows by the induction hypothesis that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
is also measurable.

(ii)

Assume that
\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
is measurable, and
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
is measurable. As
\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}=\varphi_{x}[\varphi_{x}^{(i)}/x]
, these assumptions yield
\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
measurable, and therefore
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(x)
is measurable (since it coincides with
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
). Then, since
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x})={\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)-1
, it follows by the induction hypothesis as before that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
is itself measurable.

Now since each of
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
, with
i\in\omega
, is measurable, and moreover,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})\subseteq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
for
i\in\omega
, it follows that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)=\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})
is also measurable.

(f)

\varphi=\nu x.\varphi_{x}
. In this case, the co-continuity of the operator used to define
\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V}
(see Remark
11
) gives

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)=\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x})

(13)

where
\varphi_{x}^{(0)}=\top
and
\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}=\varphi_{x}[\varphi_{x}^{(i)}/x]
for
i\in\omega
. The conclusion now follows similarly to the previous case.

Clearly, for
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
and
c\in C
, we have
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)={\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{\emptyset}(\varphi)
, where
\emptyset:\emptyset\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
is the unique (empty) valuation. Since by Proposition
33
,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{\emptyset}(\varphi)
is measurable, so is
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
.

6.
Equivalence of the Two Semantics

This section shows that the step-wise semantics and the path-based semantics for
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
coincide.

Theorem 34
.

The step-wise semantics for
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
(Definition
3
) and the path-based semantics for
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
(Definition
5
) coincide. That is, given a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
and
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}=\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
.

Proof 6.1
.

We will prove
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
and
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsupseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
.

•

The proof of
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
exploits the automata-theoretic characterisation of
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}
as the extent of the product (see Definition
2.6
) of
(C,\gamma)
and
({\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\beta,\Omega)
(Theorem
3
), where
({\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\beta,\Omega)
is the parity
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-automaton induced by the formula
\varphi
. This result assumes that
\varphi
is (clean and) strictly guarded, but as discussed in Section
3
, any formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
is equivalent to a (clean and) strictly guarded one. Now given
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
and
c\in C
, the part of the product automaton relevant to the computation of the extent
{\mathsf{e}}_{\gamma\otimes\beta}(c,\varphi)
consists of those states reachable from
(c,\varphi)
. Moreover, the automaton
({\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\beta,\Omega)
is
deterministic
(there is at most one transition labelled by any
\lambda\in\Lambda
from any state). As a result, for a reachable state
(d,\psi)
of the product automaton, the coalgebra structure is inherited from that of
d
in
(C,\gamma)
(with only transitions matching those of the formula automaton being considered), and the parity is given by
\Omega(\psi)
. An immediate consequence of this is that paths from
c
in
(C,\gamma)
satisfying
\varphi
are in one-to-one correspondence with paths from
(c,\varphi)
in
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta)
satisfying
\varphi
, with the associated transition weights also agreeing. As a result, any countable, disjoint cylinder set cover for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{(c,\varphi)}(\varphi)
in
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta)
yields a countable, disjoint cylinder set cover for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
in
(C,\gamma)
with the same measure, and conversely. This finally gives
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi))=\mu_{\gamma\otimes\beta}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{(c,\varphi)}(\varphi))
. Using this observation together with Theorem
3
and the definition of
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
, we can reduce proving
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
to proving that the following holds

\displaystyle{\mathsf{e}}_{\gamma\otimes\beta}(c,\varphi)\sqsubseteq\mu_{\gamma\otimes\beta}({\mathcal{C}})

(14)

for any countable, disjoint cylinder set cover
{\mathcal{C}}
for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{(c,\varphi)}(\varphi)
in
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta)
. The proof of (
14
) makes use of the
unfolding
of the automaton
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta,\Omega)
with initial state
(c,\varphi)
into an infinite tree. For a pointed, parity
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-automaton
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
, its
unfolding
is the pointed, parity
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-automaton
(D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime},d^{\prime}_{0})
where
D^{\prime}
contains a copy
d^{\prime}_{0}
of the initial state
d_{0}
, and for each copy
d^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}
of some
d\in D
and each transition
\textstyle{d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w,i}
\textstyle{(d_{1},\ldots,d_{j_{i}})}
in
(D,\delta)
,
(D^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
contains (new) copies
d_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,d_{j_{i}}^{\prime}
of
d_{1},\ldots,d_{j_{i}}
and a transition
\textstyle{d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w,i}
\textstyle{(d_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,d_{j_{i}}^{\prime})}
. The states of
D^{\prime}
inherit their parities from the corresponding states in
D
: if
d^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}
is a copy of
d\in D
, then
\Omega^{\prime}(d^{\prime})=\Omega(d)
.

We now write
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
for the unfolding of
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta,\Omega)
at
(c,\varphi)
, and fix a countable, disjoint cylinder set cover
{\mathcal{C}}
for the set of paths
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{(c,\varphi)}(\varphi)
in
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta)
. Using
{\mathcal{C}}
and the automaton
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
, we construct a countable collection of parity
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-automata: (i) one for each cylinder set
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, denoted
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})\!\!\restriction\!C
, whose paths are those paths in
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
which are covered by
C
, (ii) and another one, denoted
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})\setminus{\mathcal{C}}
, whose paths are those paths in
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
not covered by any
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Concretely, for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})\!\!\restriction\!C=(D^{C},\delta^{C},\Omega,d_{0}^{C})
is the sub-tree of
(D,\delta,d_{0})
which ”matches”
C
; that is, assuming
C={\mathsf{Cyl}}(q)
,
(D^{C},\delta^{C},\Omega,d_{0}^{C})
is obtained from
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
by removing, starting from the root and continuing for a number of steps equal to the depth of the path fragment
q
, transitions not covered by
q
along with the entire sub-trees having those transitions as initial transitions. Also,
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})\setminus{\mathcal{C}}=(D^{0},\delta^{0},\Omega,d_{0}^{0})
retains precisely those transitions of
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
which can be extended to a path not covered by
{\mathcal{C}}
; note that paths not covered by
{\mathcal{C}}
cannot satisfy
\varphi
, as
{\mathcal{C}}
covers all of
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{(c,\varphi)}(\varphi)
. Both
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})\!\!\restriction\!C
and
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})\setminus{\mathcal{C}}
) inherit parities from
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
. In other words, we have ”separated” the unfolding
(D,\delta,\Omega,d_{0})
of
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta,\Omega)
into a countable number of
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-automata, based on the given cover
{\mathcal{C}}
for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{(c,\varphi)}(\varphi)
.

The inequality (
14
) now follows from:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{e}}_{\gamma\otimes\beta}(c,\varphi)~=~{\mathsf{e}}_{\delta}(c,\varphi)~\sqsubseteq~\sum\limits_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\mu_{\delta^{C}}(C)~=~\sum\limits_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\mu_{\gamma\otimes\beta}(C)

where:

–

The first equality follows from the extent being preserved by the unfolding. This is because extents are preserved by
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra homomorphisms (an easy induction on the number of parities proves this), and in particular by the homomorphism sending each copy of a state to the original state.

–

The inequality above is a consequence of:

*

{\mathsf{e}}_{\delta}(c,\varphi)\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{e}}_{\delta^{0}}((c,\varphi)^{0})+\sum\limits_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\delta^{C}}((c,\varphi)^{C})
, which also follows easily by induction on the number of parities, using the definitions of extent and
\nu
-extent, and the ”splitting” of
(D,\delta,d_{0})
into
(D^{0},\delta^{0},d_{0}^{0})
and
(D^{C},\delta^{C},d_{0}^{C})
with
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

*

{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\delta^{C}}((c,\varphi)^{C})=\mu_{\delta^{C}}(C)
for each
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, which follows from the definition of
\mu_{\delta^{C}}
on cylinder sets, using the fact that
C
covers
all
paths from
(c,\varphi)^{C}
in
(D^{C},\delta^{C},\Omega,d_{0}^{C})
and nothing else),

*

the fact that the extent of an automaton with no
accepting paths
11
11

11

Here, an
accepting path
is one which satisfies the parity condition; that is, all branches of the tree representing that path are such that the largest parity occurring infinitely often along the branch is even.
(in this case
(D^{0},\delta^{0},\Omega,d_{0}^{0})
) is
0
. To see why this is true note that, whenever the extent of an automaton state is not
0
, one can construct an accepting path from that state by using the definition of extent, in particular, the relationship between the extent of a state and the extents of its successors. The proof of this is again by induction on the number of parities, and the only slight difficulty is proving that the constructed path is accepting; for this, one needs to record the tuples of ordinals (one ordinal per parity) at which the extent values of the states along the path are obtained, as per Theorem
2.5
. The reason why
(D^{0},\delta^{0},\Omega,d_{0}^{0})
has no accepting paths is that any accepting path would satisfy
\varphi
(given the construction of
(D^{0},\delta^{0},\Omega,d_{0}^{0})
from
(C\times{\mathsf{Cl}}(\varphi),\gamma\otimes\beta,\Omega)
), and would therefore be covered by
{\mathcal{C}}
.

–

The last equality is an immediate consequence of the definition of
(D^{C},\delta^{C},\Omega,d_{0}^{C})
.

This concludes the proof of the inequality
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
.

•

We now prove the converse inequality. For
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
and
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
such that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(x)
is measurable for each
c\in C
and
x\in\mathcal{V}
, we let
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}:C\to S
be given by
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}(c)=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi))
for
c\in C
. (Recall that, by Proposition
33
,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi)
is measurable.) We will prove a more general statement, namely that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}\sqsupseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}
for each
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
and each
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
such that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(x)
is measurable for each
c\in C
and
x\in\mathcal{V}
, where
\tilde{V}:\mathcal{V}\to S^{C}
is given by
\tilde{V}(x)(c)=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(x))
. For this, we again use induction on the fixpoint nesting depth of
\varphi
.

(1)

{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)=0
. We prove the statement by structural induction on
\varphi
:

(a)

\varphi=\bot
. Then:

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\bot\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}(c)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\bot))

(definition of
\llparenthesis\_\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}(\emptyset)

(
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\bot)=\emptyset
)

\displaystyle=0

(
\mu_{\gamma}
is an
S
-valued measure)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}(c)

(step-wise semantics of
\bot
)

(b)

\varphi=\top
. Then:

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\top\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}(c)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\top))

(definition of
\llparenthesis\_\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c})

(
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\top)={\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}
)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}(c)

(definition of

\displaystyle=\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}(c)

(step-wise semantics of

. In this case we have:

(definition of

)

(step-wise semantics of

)

(d)

. In this case we have:

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\llparenthesis\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}(c)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})))

(definition of
\llparenthesis\_\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup\limits_{\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\neq 0}(c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}^{V}(\varphi_{i})/c_{i}])

(definition of
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\neq 0}\mu_{\gamma}((c,\lambda)(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})[{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}^{V}(\varphi_{i})/c_{i}]))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\neq 0}\gamma(c)(\iota_{\lambda}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}))\bullet(\bullet_{i\in\{1,\ldots{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}}\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c_{i}}^{V}(\varphi_{i})))

{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)>0
. Again, we prove the statement by structural induction on

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\mu x.\varphi_{x}\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}(c)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\mu x.\varphi_{x}))

)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x}))

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x}))

(Proposition

)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\sup_{i\in\omega}\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}\cup\{x\mapsto\lambda c.\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(x))\}}(c)

\displaystyle=\sup_{i\in\omega}\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}\cup\{x\mapsto\lambda c.\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi_{x}^{(i)}))\}}(c)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\sup_{i\in\omega}\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}\}}(c)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}(c)

(characterisation of
\llbracket\mu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{\tilde{V}}_{\gamma}
as supremum of an increasing, ordinal-indexed chain, see Theorem
2.5
)

For the first inequality above, note that, by the proof of Proposition
33
(the case when
\varphi=\mu x.\varphi_{x}
),
\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
is measurable. For the second inequality above, note that, as in the proof of Proposition

, we again cannot make direct use of the induction hypothesis, since in general
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x}^{(i)})<{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)
does not hold. Thus, we need to appeal once more to an inductive proof, to show
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi_{x}^{(i)}))\sqsubseteq\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}(c)
for
i=0,1,\ldots
and
c\in C
. This follows below.

(i)

i=0
. In this case,
\varphi_{x}^{(i)}=\bot
and the statement is immediate.

(ii)

Assume that
\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi_{x}^{(i)}))\sqsubseteq\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}(c)
. We then have:

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}))

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi_{x}[\varphi_{x}^{(i)}/x]))

(definition of
\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\}}(\varphi_{x}))

(proof of Proposition
33
)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 30.19807pt\hskip-30.19807pt\hbox{$\displaystyle (outer induction hypothesis, using
{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi_{x})<{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)
)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}\cup\{x\mapsto\lambda c.\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(\varphi_{x}^{(i)}))}(c)

(inner induction hypothesis, monotonicity)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}(c)

(definition of
\varphi_{x}^{(i+1)}
)

This concludes the inner induction proof, and the proof in the case
\varphi=\mu x.\varphi_{x}
.

(f)

\varphi=\nu x.\varphi_{x}
with
\varphi_{x}\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
. The proof in this case is different from the case
\varphi=\mu x.\varphi_{x}
: although the third equality in that proof could be replaced by an inequality (as
\mu_{\gamma}(\bigcap\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n})\sqsubseteq\inf\limits_{n\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}(A_{n})
holds whenever

with

A_{0}\supseteq A_{1}\supseteq\ldots
), the inequality required in the last step of the proof does not hold – see e.g. Example
