, where for
\varphi:=\nu x.\mu y.(\langle a\rangle x\sqcup\langle b\rangle y)
, the sets
A_{0},A_{1},\ldots
are precisely the sets of paths satisfying
\varphi_{x}^{(1)},\varphi_{x}^{(2)},\ldots
, and where
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}(c_{0})=\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}(c_{0})=0
while
\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(i)}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}\}}(c_{0})=1
. As before, we assume
\llparenthesis\varphi_{x}\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V}\sqsubseteq\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}}
for each
V:\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
such that the set
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(y)
is measurable for each
c\in C
and
y\in\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}
, with
\tilde{V}:\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}\to S^{C}
given by
\tilde{V}(y)(c)=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(y))
. We also fix
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
such that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(y)
is measurable for each
c\in C
and
y\in\mathcal{V}
, and let
\tilde{V}:\mathcal{V}\to S^{C}
be given by
\tilde{V}(y)(c)=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}^{V}(y))
for
y\in\mathcal{V}
and
c\in C
. To show
\llparenthesis\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrparenthesis^{V}_{\gamma}\sqsubseteq\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{\tilde{V}}_{\gamma}
, it suffices to show that
\llparenthesis\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrparenthesis^{V}_{\gamma}
(that is,
\mu_{\gamma}(\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}})
) is a post-fixpoint of the operator
{\mathsf{o}}^{\tilde{V}}_{\varphi}:S^{C}\to S^{C}
used in the definition of
\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{\tilde{V}}_{\gamma}
. To this end, we write
{\mathsf{O}}^{V}_{\varphi}:\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
for the operator used to define
\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
. We then have:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{o}}^{\tilde{V}}_{\varphi}(\mu_{\gamma}(\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}))

\displaystyle=\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{\tilde{V}[\mu_{\gamma}(\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}})/x]}

(definition of
{\mathsf{o}}^{\tilde{V}}_{\varphi}
, see Section
2.7
)

\displaystyle\sqsupseteq\llparenthesis\varphi_{x}\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}^{V[\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}/x]}

(induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}^{V[\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}/x]}(\varphi_{x}))

(Definition
5
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}(\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V[\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}/x]})

(definition of
\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{O}}^{V}_{\varphi}(\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}))

(definition of
{\mathsf{O}}^{V}_{\varphi}
, see Section
2.7
)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma}(\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}})

(
\llbracket\nu x.\varphi_{x}\rrbracket^{V}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
is the greatest fixpoint of
{\mathsf{O}}^{V}_{\varphi}
)

This concludes the proof of the inequality
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\sqsupseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}
and also the proof of the theorem.

Theorem
34
instantiates to all our example semirings (see Example
2.1
), thereby formalising the statements of Example
3
.

The next example describes the relationship between our logics on the one hand, and LTL and the linear-time
\mu
-calculus, interpreted over both non-deterministic and probabilistic transition systems, on the other.

{exa}

Let
{\mathsf{At}}
denote a finite set of atomic propositions, and let
F:{\mathsf{Set}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
be given by
F={\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})\times{\mathsf{Id}}\simeq\coprod\limits_{A\subseteq{\mathsf{At}}}{\mathsf{Id}}
. Then, finitely-branching, non-deterministic (probabilistic) transition systems can be viewed as
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, with
S
the boolean (resp. probabilistic) semiring: such transition systems are in one-to-one correspondence with
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})\times{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
-coalgebras, and these can in turn be viewed as
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras by post-composing the coalgebra map with the strength map of the monad
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
. Concretely, if
(C,\gamma)
is the
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})\times{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
-coalgebra associated to a non-deterministic (probabilistic) transition system, then
(C,{\mathsf{st}}_{{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}}),C}\circ\gamma)
is a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra:

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})\times{\mathsf{T}}_{S}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{st}}_{{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}}),C}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}({\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})\times C)}

We immediately observe that, with the above choice of functor
F
, paths through a transition system (given by infinite sequences of states, with each pair of successive states belonging to the transition relation) are in one-to-one correspondence with paths through the associated
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra (which additionally record the atomic propositions that hold in each state along the path). In what follows we will not distinguish between the two. Also, for this choice of
F
, both the qualitative logic of Section
2.7
, interpreted over
F
-coalgebras, and the quantitative logic of Definition
3
, interpreted over
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, employ modal operators of the form
\langle A\rangle
with
A\subseteq{\mathsf{At}}
; while the use of subsets of atomic propositions as modal operators may seem surprising at first, the encoding of LTL into Büchi automata also uses automata over the alphabet
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})
. For the qualitative logic associated to the above choice of
F
, given an
F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
, the interpretation of the modal operators is as follows:

\displaystyle c\in\llbracket\langle A\rangle\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}~\text{ iff }~\pi_{1}(\gamma(c))=A\text{ and }\pi_{2}(\gamma(c))\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}^{V}

for
c\in C
and
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{C}
a valuation. As a result, the LTL next operator
\_
is recovered as
\bigsqcup\limits_{A\subseteq{\mathsf{At}}}\langle A\rangle\_
, whereas atomic propositions
a\in{\mathsf{At}}
are recovered as
\bigsqcup\limits_{A\subseteq{\mathsf{At}},A\ni a}\langle A\rangle\top
. Now recall that standard propositional operators are
not
present in our logics. As a result, a direct encoding of LTL into our qualitative logic for
F
-coalgebras, defined by induction on the structure of formulas, is not possible. That said, the presence of guarded disjunctions means that our qualitative logic for
F
-coalgebras
can
encode all of LTL: to each LTL formula
\varphi
one can associate a non-deterministic Büchi automaton
B_{\varphi}
over the alphabet
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})
, and equivalently a
deterministic
parity automaton
A_{\varphi}
over the same alphabet, in such a way that
\varphi
holds on an infinite path
p
iff
B_{\varphi}
/
A_{\varphi}
accepts the infinite word over
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})
induced by
p
12
12

12

This infinite word collects the sets of atomic propositions that hold in the states along
p
.
. Now using results in
[
CSH17
]
, the automaton
A_{\varphi}
can be associated an equivalent
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{P}}({\mathsf{At}})}
-formula. As a result, although our logics lack conjunctions and arbitrary disjunctions, they are at least as expressive as LTL. (They are in fact
more
expressive, given that the expressiveness of deterministic parity automata goes beyond that of LTL.) A similar argument shows that our qualitative logics, when instantiated to the above functor
F
, match precisely the expressiveness of the linear-time
\mu
-calculus. It is also worth recalling from Example
3
that typical linear-time properties can easily be encoded in our logics, in spite of the absence of conjunctions and arbitrary disjunctions.

We now show that existential LTL and the existential variant of the linear-time
\mu
-calculus, when interpreted over either non-deterministic or probabilistic transition systems, can be recovered as instances of our quantitative logics.

(1)

In the case of non-deterministic transition systems, we make the standard assumption that each state has at least one successor. A consequence of this is that the
\nu
-extent of each state is
1
. Then, for a non-deterministic transition system
T
viewed as a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
(with
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
), a state
c\in C
and an existential LTL/linear-time
\mu
-calculus formula
\varphi
with semantics
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{T}:C\to\{0,1\}
, writing
e(\varphi)
for the encoding of
\varphi
into our qualitative logic and using the observation that the interpretations of
\varphi
and
e(\varphi)
agree over infinite paths, we have:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}(\{p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\mid p\in\llbracket e(\varphi)\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\})=1\quad\text{iff}\quad{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\cap\llbracket e(\varphi)\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(T)}\neq\emptyset\quad\text{iff}\quad\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{T}(c)=1

where
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(T)
is the set of infinite computation paths from
c
in the transition system
T
.
Thus, we recover the existential LTL semantics
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{T}:C\to\{0,1\}
of
\varphi
. The same argument applies to the linear-time
\mu
-calculus.

(2)

In the case of probabilistic transition systems, we assume that the sum of the probabilities of outgoing transitions from each state equals
1
. As a result, the
\nu
-extent of each state is again
1
. Then, for a probabilistic transition system
T
viewed as a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
(with
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
), a state
c\in C
and an existential LTL/linear-time
\mu
-calculus formula
\varphi
with semantics
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{T}:C\to[0,1]
, again writing
e(\varphi)
for the encoding of
\varphi
into our qualitative logic and using the observation that the interpretations of
\varphi
and
e(\varphi)
agree over infinite paths, we have:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}(\{p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}\mid p\in\llbracket e(\varphi)\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}})=\mu_{\gamma}(\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(T)})=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{T}(c)

and thus we again recover the standard semantics
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{T}:C\to[0,1]
of
\varphi
over probabilistic transition systems. (Note that, by Example
5
, the definition of
\mu_{\gamma}
agrees with the definition of the measure used in the semantics of probabilistic LTL.)

7.
Expressiveness of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}

We now define (non-symmetric) semantic and logical distances between states of
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, with the latter induced by the logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, and show that the two coincide.

We begin by defining a binary operation
\oslash:S\times S\to S
by

\displaystyle t\oslash s=\sup\{u\mid u\bullet s\sqsubseteq t\}\,.

The operation
\varoslash
is thus a kind of inverse to the semiring multiplication. Assumption
2.1
ensures that
\varoslash
is well defined.

Remark 35
.

As a result of the above definition,
t\oslash s=\top
if and only if
s\sqsubseteq t
. Moreover,
(t\oslash s)\bullet s\sqsubseteq t
. The latter follows from

\displaystyle(t\oslash s)\bullet s

\displaystyle=\sup\{u\mid u\bullet s\sqsubseteq t\}\bullet s

(definition of
\oslash
)

\displaystyle=\sup\{u\bullet s\mid u\bullet s\sqsubseteq t\}

(
\bullet
preserves suprema in the first argument, by Assumption
4
)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq t

Remark 36
.

The operation
\_\varoslash s:(S,\sqsubseteq)\to(S,\sqsubseteq)
is in fact a right adjoint to
\_\bullet s:(S,\sqsubseteq)\to

(S,\sqsubseteq)
; that is,

u\bullet s\sqsubseteq t
if and only if
u\sqsubseteq t\varoslash s
. The ”only if” direction is immediate, whereas the ”if” direction follows from the monotonicity of
\bullet
in the second argument together with Remark
35
.

{exa}

(1)

When
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
,
\varoslash:S\times S\to S
instantiates to

\varominus:\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{N}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
given by

\displaystyle n\varominus m=\begin{cases}0,~\text{ if }m\geq n,\\
n-m,~\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

The above definition also applies to the bounded variants of the tropical semiring.

(2)

Similarly, when
S=([0,1],+,0,*,1)
,
\varoslash:S\times S\to S
instantiates to
\varoslash:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
given by

\displaystyle p\varoslash q=\begin{cases}1,~\text{ if }q\leq p,\\
p/q,~\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3)

When
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
,
\varoslash:S\times S\to S
can be defined using implication:

\displaystyle t\varoslash s=(s\rightarrow t)\,.

The next two properties of
\varoslash
will be useful later.

Proposition 37
.

If
r,s,t\in S
are such that
r\sqsubseteq s
, then
t\varoslash r\sqsupseteq t\varoslash s
.

Proof 7.1
.

Immediate from the definition of
\varoslash
and the monotonicity of
\bullet
in the second argument.

Proposition 38
.

The following holds:
(a\oslash b)\bullet(b\oslash c)\sqsubseteq a\oslash c
for
a,b,c\in S
.

Proof 7.2
.

The statement follows from:

\displaystyle(a\oslash b)\bullet(b\oslash c)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\sup\{u\mid u\bullet b\sqsubseteq a\}\bullet\sup\{v\mid v\bullet c\sqsubseteq b\}

(definition of
\oslash
)

\displaystyle=\sup\{u\bullet v\mid u\bullet b\sqsubseteq a,v\bullet c\sqsubseteq b\}

(Assumption
4
)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\sup\{u\bullet v\mid u\bullet v\bullet c\sqsubseteq a\}

(
u\bullet b\sqsubseteq a
and
v\bullet c\sqsubseteq b
imply
u\bullet v\bullet c\sqsubseteq a
)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\sup\{t\mid t\bullet c\sqsubseteq a\}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq a\oslash c

(definition of
\oslash
)

Corollary 39
.

Let
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i}\in S
for
i\in I
. Then the following holds:

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\oslash b_{i})\bullet\inf\limits_{i\in I}(b_{i}\oslash c_{i})\sqsubseteq\inf\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\oslash c_{i})

Proof 7.3
.

We have:

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\oslash b_{i})\bullet\inf\limits_{i\in I}(b_{i}\oslash c_{i})\sqsubseteq(a_{i}\oslash b_{i})\bullet(b_{i}\oslash c_{i})\sqsubseteq a_{i}\oslash c_{i}

for all
i\in I
, and therefore:

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\oslash b_{i})\bullet\inf\limits_{i\in I}(b_{i}\oslash c_{i})

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle\inf\limits_{i\in I}(a_{i}\oslash c_{i})

as stated.

The following property of
\varoslash
will also be needed later on.

Proposition 40
.

The operation
\varoslash:S\times S\to S
satisfies
(\inf\limits_{i\in I}t_{i})\varoslash s=\inf\limits_{i\in I}(t_{i}\varoslash s)
for
s_{i},t_{i}\in S
with
i\in I
.

Proof 7.4
.

Immediate from Remark
36
, using the fact that right adjoints preserve limits.

The notion of
partial trace
, defined next, plays a key role in our development.

{defi}

[Partial trace]
A
partial (
F
-)trace
is an element of the initial
(\{*\}+F)
-algebra
(B,\beta)
.

The notion of partial trace thus covers both (completed) finite traces and incomplete traces. It is similar to the notion of path fragment (Definition
2.2
), except that a partial trace does not record coalgebra states. (While we could have defined partial traces as an instance of Definition
2.2
, we believe a direct definition is clearer.) We write
\epsilon=\beta(\iota_{1}(*))
for the empty partial trace.

The notion of
partial trace behaviour
defined next exploits the fact that
\beta:\{*\}+FB\to B
is an isomorphism.

{defi}

[Partial trace behaviour]

The
partial trace behaviour
of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
, denoted
{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}:C\times B\to S
, is the greatest fixpoint of the operator on
{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,B}
given by

\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}(F)}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{FC,FB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{E}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{{\mathsf{T}}_{S}FC,FB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma\times{\mathsf{id}}_{FB})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,FB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma},\_]\circ i}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,\{*\}+FB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\mathsf{id}}_{C}\times\beta^{-1})^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{Rel}}_{C,B}}

where
{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}
is the
\nu
-extent of Definition
2.6
, and where
i:C\times(\{*\}+FB)\to C+C\times FB
is the isomorphism arising from the distributivity of products over coproducts in
{\mathsf{Set}}
.

Thus, similarly to the notions of maximal trace behaviour (Definition
2.5
) and finite trace behaviour (Definition
2.5
), the notion of partial trace behaviour assigns values in
S
to pairs consisting of a state in a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra and a partial
F
-trace. The operator in Definition
7
is similar to the one used to define finite trace behaviour (Definition
2.5
), while taking into account that
B
also contains incomplete traces. In particular,
{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}
assigns the value
{\mathsf{e}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}(c)
to the pair
(c,\epsilon)
.

We now observe that partial traces directly correspond to certain formulas in our logic, namely to those fixpoint-free formulas which only contain modalities of the form
\langle\lambda\rangle
with
\lambda\in\Lambda
. We let
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
consist of all such formulas.

Remark 41
.

Partial traces
b\in B
are in one-to-one correspondence with modal formulas
\varphi_{b}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}
and moreover,
{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}(c,b)=\llbracket\varphi_{b}\rrbracket_{\gamma}(c)
for all states
c
of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
. In particular, the empty partial trace
\epsilon
corresponds to the modal formula
\top
. This can be proved by an easy induction over
B
, using the specific shape of the functor
F
.

We are now ready to define both a semantic distance and a logical distance between states of
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras.

{defi}

[Linear-time distance]

The
linear-time distance

{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}:C\times D\to S
from states of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
to states of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(D,\delta)
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)=\inf\limits_{b\in B}({\mathsf{ptr}}_{\delta}(d,b)\oslash{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}(c,b))

for
c\in C
and
d\in D
.

Intuitively, the distance from
c
to
d
measures how much worse the partial trace behaviour of state
d
is compared to the partial trace behaviour of state
c
. Using Remark
35
, we have that
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)=\top
if and only if
{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}(c,b)\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\delta}(d,b)
for all
b\in B
. Informally,
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)=\top
if and only if the partial trace behaviour of
d
is ”better” than the partial trace behaviour of
c
, in that the quantity
d
associates to a partial trace is above the quantity
c
associates to that same partial trace, for every partial trace in
B
.

{exa}

When
S=(\{0,1\},\vee,0,\wedge,1)
,
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)=1=\top
if and only if state
d
can exhibit all partial traces which state
c
can exhibit. Also, when
S=(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\min,\infty,+,0)
,
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)
is the maximum difference, taken across all partial traces, between the cost of exhibiting a partial trace from state
d
in
(D,\delta)
and the cost of exhibiting the same partial trace from state
c
in
(C,\gamma)
. In particular, if
d
has a lower cost than
c
for each partial trace,
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)=0=\top
.

{defi}

[Logical distance]

Given a set
L\subseteq\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
of formulas, the
logical distance

{\mathsf{d}}^{L}_{\gamma,\delta}:C\times D\to S
from a state
c\in C
of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(C,\gamma)
to a state
d\in D
of a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra
(D,\delta)
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathsf{d}}^{L}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)=\inf\limits_{\varphi\in L}(\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\delta}(d)\oslash\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}(c))\,.

Similarly to the linear-time distance, we have that
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}^{L}(c,d)=\top
if and only if
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}(c)\sqsubseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\delta}(d)
for all
\varphi\in L
.

The next result states that both distances satisfy abstract versions of the reflexivity axiom (when restricted to a single coalgebra) and of the triangle inequality required of any pseudometric. The fact that the inequalities in (
3
) and (
4
) of Proposition
42
are reversed compared to the standard triangle inequality stems from the fact that a distance of
\top\in S
in our setting corresponds to a distance of
0\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in the standard setting. We note also that our distances are
not
symmetric when restricted to a single
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra.

Proposition 42
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
,
(D,\delta)
and
(E,\eta)
be
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, and let
L\subseteq\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\gamma}(c,c)=\top
,

(2)

{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\gamma}^{L}(c,c)=\top
,

(3)

{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}(c,d)\bullet{\mathsf{d}}_{\delta,\eta}(d,e)\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\eta}(c,e)
,

(4)

{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}^{L}(c,d)\bullet{\mathsf{d}}_{\delta,\eta}^{L}(d,e)\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\eta}^{L}(c,e)
,

for all
c\in C
,
d\in D
and
e\in E
.

Proof 7.5
.

The statements follow directly from Definitions
7
and respectively
7
, together with Corollary
39
.

Remark 43
.

Lawvere
[
Law02
]
observed that pseudometric spaces are the same as categories enriched in the quantale
(\mathbb{N}^{\infty},\geq)
, with
+
as tensor. Our use of semiring-valued distances is similar in spirit to loc. cit. – in the above quantale,
0
is the top element and a distance of
\top
corresponds to two points being equivalent. Indeed, our assumptions on the universe
S
of quantities make
(S,\sqsubseteq)
a quantale (with
\bullet
as tensor), although we choose not to expand on or exploit this observation in the present paper.

An immediate consequence of Remark
41
is that logical distance w.r.t. modal formulas in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}
coincides with the linear-time distance
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}
. This immediately gives
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}^{\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}^{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}}={\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}
. The remainder of this section shows that logical distance w.r.t. the
full
logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
also coincides with
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}
. The next two lemmas, formalising some properties of
\varoslash
, will prove useful for this.

Lemma 44
.

Let
s_{i},t_{i}\in S
for
i\in I
. Then the following holds:

\displaystyle(\inf_{i\in I}t_{i})\varoslash(\inf_{i\in I}s_{i})\sqsupseteq\inf_{i\in I}(t_{i}\varoslash s_{i})

Proof 7.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\inf_{i\in I}(t_{i}\varoslash s_{i})

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\inf_{i\in I}(t_{i}\varoslash(\inf_{j\in I}s_{j}))

(Proposition
37
)

\displaystyle=(\inf_{i\in I}t_{i})\varoslash(\inf_{j\in I}s_{j})

(Proposition
40
)

\displaystyle=(\inf_{i\in I}t_{i})\varoslash(\inf_{i\in I}s_{i})

Lemma 45
.

The following holds for
s_{i},t_{i}\in S
with
i\in\omega
:

\displaystyle(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}t_{i})\oslash(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i})\sqsupseteq\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}(t_{i}\oslash s_{i})

Proof 7.7
.

Let
u=\inf\limits_{i\in\omega}(t_{i}\oslash s_{i})
. Then,
u\sqsubseteq t_{i}\oslash s_{i}
for
i\in\omega
, and therefore, by Remark
35
and the monotonicity of
\bullet
in the second argument,
u\bullet s_{i}\sqsubseteq t_{i}
for all
i\in\omega
. We then have:

\displaystyle u\bullet\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}(u\bullet s_{i})

(Remark
15
)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}t_{i}

(see above)

and therefore, using the definition of
\oslash
,
u\sqsubseteq(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}t_{i})\oslash(\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}s_{i})
as required.

We will also use the following two lemmas to rephrase the definition of
\mu_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{M}}_{c}\to S
with
c\in C
(see Definitions
5
and
4
) on measurable sets of paths of the form
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
with
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
, by replacing cylinder set covers with covers whose elements are of the form
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
with
\psi\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
. To this end, we say that
\psi_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}

extends

\psi_{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
when the partial trace associated to
\psi_{1}
(see Remark
41
) is a prefix of that associated to
\psi_{2}
. Also, we say that
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
are
disjoint
when their associated partial traces are incompatible (i.e. they are not both prefixes of some other partial trace). Thus, two disjoint formulas in
{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
cannot both hold on a path. Disjointness then extends to pairs of formulas in
\{\bot\}\cup{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}
, with
\bot
assumed to be disjoint from any formula in
\{\bot\}\cup{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}
, including itself.

The first lemma states that the semantics, over paths in
(C,\gamma)
, of any formula in
{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
can be expressed in terms of cylinder sets.

Lemma 46
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra, and let
\psi\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
. Then, for
c\in C
,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
can be written as a finite disjoint union of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets.

Proof 7.8

(Proof (Sketch))
.

Structural induction on
\psi
, using the fact that there are finitely-many transitions from each state in
(C,\gamma)
, and thus also finitely many transitions which match a given modal operator
\langle\lambda\rangle
with
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

The second lemma shows that the semantics, over paths in
(C,\gamma)
, of any fixpoint formula
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
can be expressed in terms of the semantics of formulas in
{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 47
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra, and let
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{V}}
. Moreover, let
V:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{C}}
be such that for each
x\in V
,
V(x)
is of the form

\displaystyle\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}(\bigcup_{j\in\omega}\llbracket\varphi^{i}_{j}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}})

(16)

with the formulas
\varphi^{i}_{j}\in\{\bot\}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
for
i,j\in\omega
subject to the following conditions:

•

for each
i\in\omega
, the formulas
(\varphi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
are pairwise disjoint,

•

for each
i\in\omega
, the family
(\varphi^{i+1}_{j})_{j\in\omega}

refines
the family
(\varphi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
; that is, for each
j\in\omega
, there exists
k\in\omega
such that
\varphi^{i+1}_{j}
extends
\varphi^{i}_{k}
.

Then,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
can also be written in the above form.

Proof 7.9

(Proof (Sketch))
.

The proof is by induction of the fixpoint nesting depth of
\varphi
.

(1)

{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)=0
. We prove the statement by structural induction on
\varphi
.

(a)

For
\varphi=\top
, the statement is immediate – take
\varphi^{i}_{0}=\top
and
\varphi^{i}_{j+1}=\bot
for
i,j\in\omega
.

(b)

For
\varphi=x\in\mathcal{V}
, the statement follows from the assumption on
V
.

(c)

For
\varphi=\langle\lambda\rangle(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})
and
i\in\omega
, assume that each
\llbracket\varphi_{k}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
with
k\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
can be written as
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}(\bigcup_{j\in\omega}\llbracket(\varphi_{k})^{i}_{j}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}})
. For
i\in\omega
, let
\{\varphi^{i}_{j}\mid j\in\omega\}
contain all formulas of the form
\langle\lambda\rangle((\varphi_{1})^{i}_{j_{1}},\ldots,(\varphi_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)})^{i}_{j_{{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)}}))
with
j_{k}\in\omega
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
. Clearly, for each
i\in\omega
, the formulas
(\varphi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
satisfy the two conditions in the statement of the lemma, given that each family
((\varphi_{k})^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
with
k\in\{1,\ldots,{\mathsf{ar}}(\lambda)\}
does so. The fact that (
16
) holds can also be easily proved.

(d)

The case of modalities incorporating disjunctions (this also covers the case
\varphi=\bot
) is treated similarly.

(2)

{\mathsf{fnd}}(\varphi)>0
. As for case (
1
), the statement follows by structural induction on
\varphi
, with four similar sub-cases treated in the same way, but with two additional cases, considered below:

(e)

For
\varphi=\mu x.\varphi_{x}
, just like in the proof of Proposition
33
, we cannot directly apply the induction hypothesis. Instead, we use the equality (
12
) to reduce showing that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
can be written in the required form to showing that each of
\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(n)}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V}\}}
, with
n\in\omega
, can be written in this form. Once this is shown (by an easy induction on
n
), the resulting families
(\varphi_{x}^{n})^{i}_{j}
with
i,j\in\omega
and
n\in\omega
can be used to obtain the required
(\varphi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega,i\in\omega}
. For this, the observation that, for fixed
i\in\omega
and
n\in\omega
, the family
(\varphi_{x}^{n+1})^{i}_{j}
with
j\in\omega
contains
13
13

13

once formulas containing
\bot
have been identified with
\bot
the family
(\varphi_{x}^{n})^{i}_{j}
with
j\in\omega
, is used; this, in turn, follows from
\varphi
not containing unguarded occurrences of any fixpoint variables.

(f)

The proof in the case when
\varphi=\nu x.\varphi_{x}
is similar and uses the equality (
13
) to reduce showing that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
can be written in the required form to showing that each of
\llbracket\varphi_{x}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V\cup\{x\mapsto\llbracket\varphi_{x}^{(n)}\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}^{V}\}}
, with
n\in\omega
, can be written in this form. In this case, the observation that, for fixed
i\in\omega
and
n\in\omega
, the family
(\varphi_{x}^{n+1})^{i}_{j}
with
j\in\omega
refines the family
(\varphi_{x}^{n})^{i}_{j}
with
j\in\omega
, is used; this, again, is a consequence of guardedness.

{exa}

Let
F=\{a,b\}\times{\mathsf{Id}}
.

•

For
\varphi=\mu x.\nu y.(\langle a\rangle x\sqcup\langle b\rangle y)
, capturing the property that only finitely-many
a
-labelled transitions are present,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
is given by the intersection
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}
where, for
i\in\omega
,
A_{i}=\bigcup\limits_{k\in\omega,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}}\llbracket\langle b\rangle^{n_{1}}\langle a\rangle\langle b\rangle^{n_{2}}\langle a\rangle\ldots\langle b\rangle^{n_{k}}\langle a\rangle\langle b\rangle^{i}\top\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
. In other words, for
i\in\omega
, the family
(\varphi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
contains those formulas in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}
whose associated partial trace contains at least
i

consecutive

b
-transitions.

•

For
\varphi=\nu x.\mu y.(\langle a\rangle x\sqcup\langle b\rangle y)
, capturing the property that infinitely-many
a
-labelled transitions are present,
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
is given by the intersection
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}A_{i}
where, for
i\in\omega
,
A_{i}=\bigcup\limits_{n_{0},\ldots,n_{i-1}\in\mathbb{N}}\llbracket\langle b\rangle^{n_{0}}\langle a\rangle\langle b\rangle^{n_{1}}\langle a\rangle\ldots\langle b\rangle^{n_{i-1}}\langle a\rangle\top\rrbracket_{\zeta^{\prime}_{C}}
. In other words, for
i\in\omega
, the family
(\varphi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
contains those formulas in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1}
whose associated partial trace contains at least
i

a
-labelled transitions.

We now have the following result.

Proposition 48
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
be a
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebra, and let
c\in C
and
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
. Then:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi))=\inf\{\sum\limits_{i\in\omega}\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi_{i}))\mid(\varphi_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}^{1})_{i\in\omega}\text{ pairwise disj.},\,{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi_{i})\}

Proof 7.10
.

Recall that

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}({\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi))=\inf\{\mu_{\gamma}({\mathcal{C}})\mid{\mathcal{C}}\text{ is a countable, disjoint cylinder set cover for ${\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)$}\}\,.

Clearly, the lhs in the statement of Proposition
48
is
\sqsubseteq
the rhs – by Lemma
46
, countable covers for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
made of pairwise disjoint sets of the form
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
with
\psi\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
yield countable covers made of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets, with the same measure.

To show that the lhs is
\sqsupseteq
the rhs, let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a countable cover for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
consisting of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets. We will use
{\mathcal{C}}
to construct a countable cover for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
consisting of pairwise disjoint sets of the form
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
with
\psi\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
. To this end, let
\Psi=\{\psi\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}\mid\emptyset\neq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}\}
, and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}=\{{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)\mid\psi\in\Psi\}
. Note that, since
(C,\gamma)
is finitely branching,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
is countable. Clearly,
\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
. We will now show that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
covers
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
. Assume that this is not the case; that is, there exists
p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
such that
p\not\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
for any
\psi\in\Psi
. By
(Z_{C},\zeta_{C})
being a final
C\times F
-coalgebra, with
Z_{C}
obtained as an
\omega^{\mathsf{op}}
-limit, there exist cylinder sets
C^{p}_{i}
of depth uniformly
i
, with
i\in\omega
, such that
\{p\}=\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}C^{p}_{i}
. Remark
41
now yields a formula
\psi_{i}=\psi_{C^{p}_{i}}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
(which corresponds to the underlying partial trace of
C^{p}_{i}
) for each
i\in\omega
. We immediately obtain
\psi_{i}\not\in\Psi
(since
p\in C^{p}_{i}\subseteq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi_{i})
) for
i\in\omega
. We now use the fact that
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)=\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}\bigcup\limits_{j\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j})
, with
\varphi^{i}_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
for
i,j\in\omega
(see Lemma
47
) to derive a contradiction. From this and
p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
we obtain a decreasing sequence
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{0}_{j_{0}})\supseteq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{1}_{j_{1}})\supseteq\ldots
with
p\in\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})
. Moreover,
\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}}\not\in\Psi
(since
p\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})
) for any
i\in\omega
. As a result,
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})\not\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
for any
i\in\omega
. It then follows that
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})\not\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
(see below). This, in turn, gives
p^{\prime}\in(\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}}))\setminus(\bigcup{\mathcal{C}})
. Moreover,
p^{\prime}
is
F
-behaviourally equivalent to
p
(given the shape of the
\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}}
s and the fact that both
p
and
p^{\prime}
belong to
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})
for
i\in\omega
), which now gives
p^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
(as behaviourally equivalent states in
(Z_{C},\zeta_{C})
satisfy the same formulas of
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
). We have therefore derived a contradiction, since
p^{\prime}\not\in\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
and therefore
p^{\prime}\not\in{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
. This concludes the proof that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
covers
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
. We have thus constructed a countable cover for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
, with elements of the form
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
with
\psi\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}_{\Lambda}
. The last step is to provide a disjoint sub-cover of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
, which still covers
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
; this can be done by first replacing each
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
for which
\psi\in\Psi
is
not
of uniform depth by a finite collection of sets of the form
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
with
\psi
of uniform depth, and then removing from
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
all sets
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi^{\prime})
for which there exists
\psi\in\Psi
with
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi^{\prime})\subsetneq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\psi)
. This transformation preserves the measure of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
. Also, the resulting disjoint cover
{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}
for
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi)
has measure below that of
{\mathcal{C}}
(as
\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}_{\Psi}\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
). As a result, the lhs in the statement of Proposition
48
is
\sqsupseteq
the rhs.

It remains to prove the earlier claim that, if
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})\not\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
for
i\in\omega
, then
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}{\mathsf{Paths}}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})\not\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
. For this, note that by Lemma
46
, each
{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})
is a
finite
union of pairwise disjoint cylinder sets (which we can assume w.l.o.g. are of depth uniformly
i
– if not, simply replace them by finite unions of cylinder sets of depth uniformly
i
), at least one of which is not included in
\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
. This allows us to construct a decreasing chain
C_{0}\supseteq C_{1}\supseteq\ldots
of cylinder sets with
C_{i}\subseteq{\mathsf{Paths}}_{c}(\varphi^{i}_{j_{i}})
and
C_{i}\not\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
for
i\in\omega
. Then,
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}C_{i}\not\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
. For, if this was not the case, since
\bigcap\limits_{i\in\omega}C_{i}=\{p^{\prime\prime}\}
with
p^{\prime\prime}\in Z_{C}
(due to the shape of the
C_{i}
s), we would have
p^{\prime\prime}\in\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
, and therefore
p^{\prime\prime}\in C
for some
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. But this would give
C_{i}\subseteq C
for some
i\in\omega
, and therefore
C_{i}\subseteq\bigcup{\mathcal{C}}
, which contradicts the definition of the
C_{i}
s. This concludes the proof.

We finally show that the logical distance w.r.t.
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
and the linear-time distance coincide.

Theorem 49
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
and
(D,\delta)
be two
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras. Then,
{\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}={\mathsf{d}}_{\gamma,\delta}^{\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}}
.

and

(\varphi_{i})_{i\in\omega}

As a corollary, using Remark
35
, we obtain the following logical characterisation of (a generalised version of) partial trace inclusion.

Corollary 50
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
and
(D,\delta)
be two
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, and let
c\in C
and
d\in D
. Then,
{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}(c,b)\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\delta}(d,b)
for all
b\in B
if and only if
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}(c)\sqsubseteq\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\delta}(d)
for all
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
.

A similar result can be stated for partial trace equivalence. This provides a semantic characterisation of the logical equivalence between states induced by the logic
\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
.

Corollary 51
.

Let
(C,\gamma)
and
(D,\delta)
be two
{\mathsf{T}}_{S}\circ F
-coalgebras, and let
c\in C
and
d\in D
. Then,
{\mathsf{ptr}}_{\gamma}(c,b)={\mathsf{ptr}}_{\delta}(d,b)
for all
b\in B
if and only if
\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\gamma}(c)=\llparenthesis\varphi\rrparenthesis_{\delta}(d)
for all
\varphi\in\mu{\mathcal{L}}_{\Lambda}
.

8.
Conclusions and Future Work

We described a generic approach to defining linear-time fixpoint logics for state-based systems that incorporate branching behaviour, and proved the equivalence of the step-wise semantics for these logics with an alternative path-based semantics, akin to those employed by existing linear-time logics. This required generalising standard measure-theory concepts and results to measures valued in a partial semiring. Our approach is uniform in this semiring, can recover standard path-based semantics for existing logics interpreted over non-deterministic and probabilistic systems (see Example
6
), and also instantiates to new settings (resource-aware systems and tree-shaped linear behaviours). The equivalence of the two semantics motivates the use of the term ”linear-time” to describe our logics. We also introduced a semantic notion of linear-time distance between states of coalgebras with branching, and showed that this coincides with a logical distance induced by our logics.

Future work will explore generalising these results to coalgebras of type
F\circ{\mathsf{T}}_{S}
(as considered e.g. in
[
JSS15
]
) and beyond. We will also study extensions of our logics that incorporate a notion of
offset
(in the case of the tropical semiring, this would instantiate to resource gains), as considered in
[
Cîr19
]
, as well as extensions to coalgebraic types which incorporate multiple types of branching.
